# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 2 OF 86

## A.19. Complementarity and coherence

Does the project complement other climate finance funding (e.g. GEF, AP, CIF, etc.) in Jordan?

No.

### A.20. Executing Entity information

The Ministry of Water and Irrigation (MWI), the Ministry of Agriculture (MoA) and the Ministry of Environment (MoE) are the National Designated Authority (NDA).

## A.21. Executive summary (max 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

### i. Climate Change Risks

Jordan is facing severe climate change risks and business as usual will no longer be an option for Jordan to adapt to its development and growth. Jordan is one of the most water scarce countries in the world and climate change further exacerbating aridity by increasing temperatures, reducing rainfall and increasing evaporation rates. This has led to unprecedented population increases due to both natural growth and refugee influx. In addition, climate change has increased the frequency and intensity of extreme weather events. The number of days with tripled rain since Jordan in 1986, compared to a doubled worldwide, exposing the population to loss of life and property damage. Climate change also leads to more frequent droughts, which cause variation on availability of water resources, water demand for agriculture and domestic purpose and existence of food security. The areas most affected by climate change are those where the cultivated land is ranked – are the most vulnerable especially in rural areas where adaptation deficit is the highest.

### ii. Key Challenges

Jordan’s economy and society have faced significant shocks in the past few years due to the ongoing conflicts in Syria and Iraq, the country's main trading partners which disrupted Jordan’s trade routes and capital inflows. The Syrian refugee crisis put considerable pressure on the country’s resources and infrastructure. In addition, the economic downturn and financial indicators weakened, the fiscal deficit rose, and financing many key priorities became very difficult. This leads to a significant increase in poverty levels and food insecurity. Climate change is likely to be a major setback which will put considerable pressure on the limited resources of the country and impact its revenue sources.

### iii. Proposed Intervention

Given the exposure, vulnerability and adaptation deficit reported by the country in their National Communication to the UN Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) and in the National Adaptation Plan (NAP), four Governorates in the Dead Sea Basin namely Karak, Ma’an, Tafila and Aqaba are considered as the most vulnerable. These Governorates host 840,900 inhabitants that face a high risk of water scarcity, food insecurity, poor access to health services, limited agriculture, poverty rate of rural population, and high climate change adaptation deficit.

### iv. The Project

The project is designed to address these challenges in Jordan through improving water use efficiency in the agriculture sector (BIRC/CC) is increase climate resilience by increasing the capacity for climate information services and climate policy (2013-2020) and the National Adaptation Plan (2020) the project is designed to increase the resilience of the four governorates in Jordan’s Dead Sea Basin. The components of the project will have three interrelated components which will work synergistically to address key risks and enhance the impact of project investments. The components are designed to deal with the lack of infrastructure, institutional capacity and public awareness on climate change adaptation include: (i) Component 1: Climate Resilient Water Systems for enhanced water security; (ii) Component 2: Climate Information Services for improved climate resilience; and (iii) Component 3: Scaling-up climate adaptation into policy and across actors (institutions, private sector, civil society). Building with learned lessons learned in Jordan as well as the GCF’s “Service

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 3 OF 86

#### v. The project will be executed jointly by FAO, UNDP, Ministry of Environment (MoE), Ministry of Agriculture (MoA), Ministry of Water and Irrigation (MWI) indicating the strong collaborative and partnership arrangements that exist between these institutions. These institutions have a long history of working together in Jordan and their strong experience and convening power that both FAO and UNDP have established in the country make it a valued partner. The project builds on the establishment institutional arrangements for climate change adaptation at national level through the National Climate Change Coordination Committee (NCC) and the Working Group on Climate Adaptation.

#### vi. The Project is the first and only country specific project which the Ministry of Environment (MoE) has included in its GCF country portfolio. It will be implemented during the first replenishment period of the GCF (2014-2017). The Project is estimated to reach 212,416 people directly and 358,900 indirectly. This basis of the value for money metrics of the Project shows that the project investment is highly justified because it has an Economic Internal Rate of Return of 24.1%. The Net Present Value was estimated to be USD 80.7 million. The Project will contribute up to 35% reduction in water demand, up to 69% reduction in groundwater overdraft and to contribute up to 4-5% to the water management goals in the National Water Sector Strategy (NWSS). This is equivalent to a reduction of 102.7 million cubic meters (mcm) in a 10-year period and 5.49 mcm for the project’s lifespan. Although in absolute terms, this seems like a small amount, it represents a significant contribution towards national water security.

In addition, 10,600 hectares of agricultural land area will be made more resilient with climate-adaptive measures that will reduce the vulnerability to drought and other extreme weather events. These benefits come from alternative sources such as the recycled one as well as from new farm management practices that will allow farmers to cope with water related stresses that are related to climate change (i.e. reduced rainfall, increased evaporation rates).

#### vii. Total project costs are estimated to be USD 32.5 million. This project is based on a request for a grant from the GCF of USD 25 million (75% of total project cost). The Government of Jordan is committed to co-financing the remaining 25%, which will amount to USD 8.1 million (USD 6.3 million and USD 1.06 million respectively, representing 6% of total costs). The beneficiaries are expected to provide USD 4.8 million for investments at the household level.

# B.1 Climate Change (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

## 1. The Hashemite Kingdom of Jordan is one of the most water scarce countries in the world: Water scarcity is driven primarily by the arid and semi-arid climatic regime as 92% of the country receives average precipitation of 135 mm/year (WB, 2006; GoJ, 2013). The average annual air temperature in the country is 14°C. The climate has become more extreme over time with a significant increase in the frequency and intensity of rainfall patterns, increased temperatures and an unprecedented population increase due to both natural growth and migration (9% vs. 5%) as well as more farmers are abandoning their fields due to the impossibility of irrigation. This is leading to a decrease in agricultural production and food security. The country has lost out of 181 countries for climate vulnerability. After some years of slight and consistent improvement, especially from 2009-2013 (UNCCD, 2014; WB, 2016), the situation has deteriorated significantly to the current 85 (NGOAN, 2018). Jordan is facing severe climate change risks and the business as usual will no longer suffice in addressing the challenges the country faces to its development and sustainability. Climate change can have a significant impact on water availability for both rural and urban households and can negatively impact their safety and health, livelihoods, and sustainable development.

## 2. Recently (WB, 2020; GoJ, 2019; USAID, 2017; UNCCD, 2014; FAO, 2020) climate change has already impacted Jordan: annual maximum and minimum temperature have increased by 0.3-1.8°C and 0.4-1.6°C respectively (WB, 2020). The mean annual air temperature in the country is projected to increase by 2.5°C by 2050. Kendall rank test trend and linear regression trends indicate that the mean, maximum and minimum air temperatures have increased significantly over time (3 mm/decade from 1986-2014). The annual rainfall precipitation tends to decrease significantly over time (-3 mm/decade in 2005-, -1.54 in 2005–12 and -1.28 in 2005–17) (RCP 8.5). The average annual air temperature has increased by about 0.6°C per decade during the last 10 years and a 2.30 mm/century per decade reduction in average annual precipitation since 1900. Data from local meteorological stations (1950–2019) as well as from remote sensing analysis (FAO, 2020) indicate that the mean annual air temperature has increased by about 0.6°C and rainfall has decreased for both temperature (MRH and MAX) and rainfall; highlighting also that rainfall is not only reduced but its patterns are changing. The decrease in rainfall is expected to be more pronounced during winter months, which is exacerbated by climate change. According to FAO, around 85% of the renewable water resources of the country come from rainfall (GoJ, 2013). The Ministry of Water and Irrigation (MWI) estimated water availability at 2,100 million cubic meters (MCM), and water availability per capita was 694 m³/year in 1950. According to the National Water Strategy 2008-2022, water availability per capita was 3600 m³/year in 1994, but fell to 145 m³/year by 2008 with a current of 93 m³/year in 2020.

## The changes - unprecedented and rapid - have had significant impacts on the agricultural sector. Reduction of chilling time (predicted to be insufficient for fruits already in 2021) (WB, 2013), reduced water availability during farming operations (about 30 days long dry season) (UNCCD, 2014; FAO, 2020), and the increase of evaporation rates (GoJ, 2013). The agricultural sector has been affected since 2013-2020 (Quattromini, 2018). Field crops, such as wheat and barley*, are highly sensitive to climate change. According to FAO, the average yield of wheat in Jordan is projected to decrease by about 5% for water availability (Al-Batin, 2013); it is projected that there will be a decrease in yield varying from 7% to 21% for barley* due to climate change and water scarcity. The decline in agricultural production has an impact on food production (one of the most diffuse commodity in Jordan), the potential decrease in yield derived from climate

change intensity varies from 5% to 10% with high evidence on the oil quality reduction UNDP,2019.

4. For many vegetable crops, high temperatures may decrease quality parameters, such as soil solubles and tenderness. It is anticipated that a 1°C or 2°C increase in temperature will decrease vegetables yield by 5 and 10%, respectively (AEG, 2012; UNESCO, 2014; GoJ-NAP, 2020). Similarly, on average, for every 1°C increase in temperature, the duration of flowering period is reduced by two bud flower induction, higher fruit drop, faster volume growth of fruit, earlier maturation, less total soluble solids and lower sugar content (AEG, 2012; UNESCO, 2014). The yield response to climate change may be more closely coupled to changes in soil resources than to immediate physiological responses of plants to temperature. This is because the availability of water for crop growth is strongly influenced by precipitation in drier systems such as the arid and semiarid rangelands of Jordan would reduce productivity UNDP,2019.

5. According to all scenarios in the Representative Concentration Pathways (RCP) the described climate change adverse trends will further deteriorate. Reportedly, WBG 2020; GoJ-NP, 2018; USAID, 2017; UNFCCC, 2014; FAO, 2013), it is extremely likely that Jordan will experience in the 2030-2100 period a temperature increase of between 1.5°C and 6.5°C (for RCPs 8.5 or 4.5) by 2030, 2.5°C to 7.5°C (for RCPs 8.5 or 4.5) by 2050, 1.9°C to 5.8°C and 2.5°C (by RCPs 4.5 or 1.5°C) by 2065; 2.6°C to 5.3°C and 4.0°C to 7.0°C (by RCPs 8.5 or 4.5) by 2090, and 2.8°C to 6.1°C and 4.0°C to 8.0°C (for RCPs 8.5 or 4.5) by 2100 (WBG, 2020; GoJ-NP, 2020; USAID, 2017; UNFCCC, 2014; FAO, 2013). The temperature increase will result in increased evaporation and this, combined with decreased rainfall intensity and frequency, will lead to a decrease of the available water resources and groundwater reserves. Standardized precipitation indices indicate how Jordan will experience more drought events during the next decades. It is expected that Jordan will experience more frequent and longer dry periods, drier days and projected evaporation and the delivery of rainfall in shorter and more intense periods are likely to increase (UNDP, 2019). The combination of these factors will lead to a decrease in water scarcity in Jordan will worsen due to climate change. National policies [GoJ-NAP, 2020; Contaz, 2017; OER, 2016; MWE, 2015] and studies recognize that the agricultural sector as well as society is vulnerable to climate change with high exposure and low adaptive capacity. In this long term, this is likely to cause serious soil degradation that could lead to desertification and worsening the situation of food security in Jordan. The National Adaptation Plan (NAP) states that the agricultural sector has a relatively low adaptive capacity, given that most of the current adaptation measures are not being implemented or are not fully effective. Furthermore, the NAP attests that the agricultural sector nor the private sector have access to the necessary resources and technologies for climate change adaptation. The lack of planned and needed adaptation measures require investments that neither the state nor the private sector can afford. In addition, the NAP states that there is a need to improve the capacity of communities and access to finance is difficult for farmers to secure because of the perceived risk inherent in agriculture incomes and the collateral requirements which many farmers are unable to offer.

6. Jordan has experienced a dramatic increase in flash floods hazards and frequency since the 1980s, compared to a drier world wide. Floods and drought both present major risks. In winter, heavy rains can cause serious flooding and landslides. The risk of flooding is common in Jordan and can be exacerbated by poor land use planning and infrastructure. Increased productivity and economic development in rural areas with lack of preventing infrastructure (e.g., dams, dikes, floodgates, drainage systems) has increased the vulnerability to flash floods and production sites) in areas at risk along rivers, valleys and in flood plains [World Bank, 2018]. Flash flooding is a major hazard for Jordan. The number of people affected by flash floods has increased over time and affected 24,321 lives. Additionally, floods usually leave vast agricultural areas covered with water (UNDP, 2019). Floods in Jordan have occurred on average every year with a frequency of 2.43 per 10 years [IBS, 2020; GFDRR, 2020; CR, 2020; WFP, 2019; UNDP, 2019].

7. In terms of drought, several studies have shown the increase incidence of drought events in Jordan.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND-FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 6 OF 86

Literature suggests that the country will face frequent non-uniform drought periods in an irregular repetitive manner [GuJ-MAP, 2020] while the World Bank estimated that Jordan has already developed perennial drought conditions [IBR, 2019]. Drought severity, magnitude and frequency impact the Jordanian agriculture system [A. Quinnie, 2010; Selja, 2013-2018]. Based on historical observational data from the Ministry of Water and Irrigation [MOIW], the number of drought months per year has increased significantly since 1990 (Figure 1). The frequency of Moderate Droughts (MDD) is increasing at the country level since 1990 at a pace of 8.13 months per 10 years for Moderate Droughts and 25.76 months per 10 years for Severe Droughts [MOIW, 2017]. The frequency of Extreme Droughts is increasing at the country level since 1990 at a pace of 38.4 months per 10 years.

### Figure 1: Occurrences of meteorological droughts in months in the Dead Sea Basin (in the series of September, 2017)

#### 10. The Dead Sea Basin covers four of the 15 sub-basins in the country. This, combined with poverty and high temperatures, has contributed to the overall increase in vulnerability in the region. The Dead Sea basin has an average temperature of about 34°C during summer months (June–August) and a mean annual temperature of approximately 18°C. Climate change projections (utilizing the models CSIROMK3L, ECHAM5MOM, HADGEM2) of the Dead Sea Basin indicate that by 2070 there will be an increase in average maximum temperatures from about 40°C to over 46°C and a significant precipitation decline from -5% to -10% by 2030 and -10% to -20% by 2050 (SABER, 2013). Water availability in the Dead Sea Basin is expected to decrease by about 70% by 2050. The basin has a total area of 486 km² and a total water extraction of 90 mm, which represented one of the highest relative deficits of all basins (SABER, 2013).

#### 11. Given the reported exposure, vulnerability and adaptation deficit of rural communities as well as the distribution of international aid across the country (mostly concentrated in the northern governorates due to higher poverty levels), the Ministry of Agriculture has identified the Dead Sea Basin as one of the most vulnerable areas for engagement processes led by the NDA and FAO identified the Dead Sea Basin as one of the most vulnerable areas and selected it for climate adaptation (Annex 2, pages 39-51). Within the basin, the project will be executed in four governorates: Karkur, Tafila, Karak and Irbid. The main impacts on agriculture are expected to be adverse impacts in the governors related to an increase in average maximum and minimum temperatures, decrease in precipitation, reduction of water availability, and increased frequency of droughts.

#### 12. The four Governorates host 840,900 inhabitants (about 9% of the total population) that are the most vulnerable in the country to climate change due to high dependency on rainfall agriculture, poverty rates in rural areas, low levels of education and literacy among women, and limited access to basic services. Additionally, the share of the rural population in the four Governorates is significantly higher than the average national level (50% compared with 36%). The majority of the rural population lives in governorates that are more urbanized and on a national level 19% of the rural population is classified as poor (WB, 2018). In Karak, Tafila and Irbid, the share of the rural population living below poverty lines is present in the target governorates. In six Ma'arif schools in Karak, Tafila and Irbid, the majority of students are from poor families (UNDP, 2015; WB, 2018). Those who live in these areas have limited access to basic services such as education, health care, water supply and sanitation. Agriculture is furthermore poorer than the ones working in other sectors especially those depending on rainfall for their production. The main impacts of climate change are expected to be adverse impacts related to changes in rainfall patterns, reduction in precipitation and the increasing frequency of droughts (FAO, 2018).

#### 13. Jordan has displayed strong ownership and commitment to changing the business as usual operations. Since 2009, when the first National Communication was developed, the policy framework for


---
**Page 16**
---

adaptation, Jordan took a rapid expansion in scope, Jordan has developed several key policies and strategic documents with regards to climate change. These include (i) the National Climate Change Policy (2014), (ii) the National Adaptation Plan (NAP) (2015), (iii) the Water Sector Strategy (WSS) for the first time in Jordan (2014), and (iv) its Intended Nationally Determined Contribution (INDC) 2015 and the National Adaptation Plan (2020). These reaffirm the Government's priorities to bring about transformative change, including through the implementation of the NAP. The NAP is being developed in accordance with a National Committee on Climate Change (NCCC) with the participation of key stakeholders to oversee the process and ensure that it is representative and inclusive. Nonetheless, although Jordan plays an active role in the climate change international fora and the country is committed to implementing its NDCs, there are several challenges facing Jordan's efforts to address climate change, including on water and agriculture, have remained, limited in ambition and action due to (i) lack of understanding of the implications and costs of adaptation measures; (ii) lack of awareness about long lasting investments. (iii) The issue is not viewed as a priority (compared to e.g. employment), in part due to a lack of understanding of its implications and costs and of the benefits of action; (iv) Lack of understanding of the linkages between climate change, water resources management, agriculture, links, common purpose and mainstreaming across institutions and levels (central vs local institutions); (v) Limited capacity for implementation, including limited institutional capacity, lack of availability of technical skills and resources as additional barriers to implementing projects in the agriculture and water sectors.

14. Through the Intended Nationally Determined Contribution (INDC - 2015) and the National Adaptation Plan (NAP, 2020), the Government has prioritized a number of priority interventions to adapt to climate change, including improving water use efficiency; promote water harvesting techniques (e.g. rainwater harvesting including flood); rainwater harvesting from rooftops; constructing multi-dams for irrigation and drinking purposes; increasing the capacity of existing dams; reducing the amount of treated wastewater in irrigation; establishing of sea water desalination units and using treated water in agriculture; improving the efficiency of irrigation systems and introducing water saving technologies such as drip, irrigation and hydroponics; promoting the use of drought-resistant crops; diversifying agricultural production; increasing farmers' incomes through crop diversification, etc. There is a strong focus in Government plans on increasing women's participation in decision making processes and income generating activities to address current gender inequalities. It is expected that depletion of natural resources and decreasing agricultural productivity will have an adverse impact on the livelihoods of rural communities and their ability to make decision making processes and income generating activities. Therefore, promoting gender equality and empower women is a necessary program in climate change adaptation plan. The Government however, does not have any specific plans or programs for this purpose at present.

15. There are five lines of projects designed to assist the country to deal with issues of climate change being implemented by UN agencies. Some key projects include those being implemented by the UNFCCC Adaptation Fund which plots innovative technology transfer and policy support. FAO and UNDP both have one project each, funded through the Global Environment Facility (GEF) and the GEF Small Grants Program. The MADAD project, investments in aquaponic and hydroponics, etc. UNDP is assisting the Government with implementing a project to improve water management and flood risk management in Jordan through an "Early Warning System for Flash Floods" in Petra and Wadi Musa as part of SDC support to the Petra Development and Tourism Region (PDT). The GEF Small Grants Program is supporting the development of capacities in local development initiatives. The Adaptation Fund of the UNFCCC (2016-2020) is investing USD 9.2 million in subduction of fresh water with wastewater for specific purposes. It is also assisting in developing a national early warning system for flash floods and droughts. The GEF Small Grants Program has invested in climate change adaptation to protect human health (2016-2014) financed by the GEF SCF fund.

16. There are also other donors undertaking investments in the area of adaptation in the country which will complement the project efforts. IFAD collaborated with the National Agriculture Research Center (NARC) and the Jordanian Ministry of Agriculture to implement a USD 2 million project to improve water use efficiency. AFD is investing (2021-2025) USD 15.2 to integrate climate-resilient agriculture in selected value chains. AFD and KfW are investing Euro 450 million in a Water Sector Policy Loan which includes diffusion of water harvesting technologies, among other activities. The World Bank is implementing the Water Sector Policy Loan (WSPL). GIZ is investing USD 2 million for removing barriers to Climate Change Adaptation in Jordan. This project will support the implementation of climate change adaptation measures that increased cost recovery and the installation of wastewater treatment plants that supply reclaimed water for irrigation purposes. Other projects and initiatives are being implemented in the Jordan Valley and in the northern governorates (Annex 2, pages 76-97).

**Page 18**
---

17\. The key barriers in adapting to climate change in the country stem from constraints at the institutional, household and policy level. Government resources and capacity to invest in the water sector are limited. At the institutional level, there is a lack of coordination between sectors that face adverse impacts such as decline in ground water recharge, stream flow reduction, increase in droughts and floods, and increased temperatures. The NAP also identified general weaknesses in public service delivery, including poor planning and implementation capacity, weak governance, lack of effective financial management and lack of clearly defined institutional roles and mandates. The private sector is limited by the lack of access to finance, information on climate risks, appropriate technologies and skills. At the household level the key barriers include the limited capacity to invest in addressing climate induced water scarcity in the water and agriculture sectors as well as the lack of knowledge about how best to adapt to climate change. There are also limited extension services of the farmers about how to best adapt farming practices to reduced and uncertain precipitation and increased temperatures. The government extension staff have limited outreach and reliance on traditional extension approaches and lack of update knowledge of the range of options available for adaptation. At the policy level, there is a lack of access to information technology (ICT) to disseminate information that can help in adaptation. Due to social and economic barriers such as poverty, women are not able to participate fully in decision making on climate resilience to climate risks; women are seldom consulted or involved in key decision although they play an important role in managing key sectors which are impacted by climate risks.

18\. An underlying cross-cutting approach of the project, based on the lessons outlined in the NAP, will be to build farmers experiences on climate change adaptation. This will allow to break down some of the barriers that exist at household level and also to create a more enabling environment for adaptation to changes in behavior and to a society and economy that is better prepared for climate change and its impacts. The project will focus on three key areas: (i) increasing access to information, (ii) building the NAP process is limited the interest and engagement of the private sector in supplying the full range of inputs and services required by farmers; (iii) improving the capacity of farmers to adapt through the use of technologies such as drip and sprinkler irrigation systems and equipment for renewable energy, they could be more actively engaged and it appears that there are several barriers that inhibit their role which include limited access to finance, information on climate risks, appropriate technologies and skills. The enabling environment and market conditions and changing regulation which increase the risk for the private sector (NAP) is however gaining increased awareness of the benefits in engaging in climate-related investment opportunities.

19\. Based on these barriers a set of interventions were selected which are accorded the highest priority based on the multi-criteria decision analysis adopted under the NAP process that ranked and weighted them based on their ability to address key barriers. The project components are designed to address these barriers and were grouped into three components which represent the pathways designed to address key barriers to climate change adaptation in the country. These pathways are designed to ensure that there these pathways can help the country address climate change risks. The project components are designed to reflect these pathways and ensure long-term sustainable change in the country. The theory of change for this project is based on the following assumptions: (i) IFAD, UNDP, the World Bank, USAID, GIZ have established from its investments across several regions and countries that a successful project can be replicated at scale; (ii) the project will provide a replicable means to address the specific constraints in the project area as well as scale-up and replicate the innovations introduced by the project to the rest of the country. The project is designed to assist the country work at the

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 PAGE 12 OF 86

## Investments in infrastructure and technical capacity for planning at the level of the Hydrological Basin (HB) are critical to address water scarcity. Governments will have to invest in enabling its institutions and people make a significant shift in their approach and behavior patterns. Government can work with communities, farmers, and other stakeholders to build resilience by investing in infrastructure such as roof top water harvesting and use of reclaimed water[1] to address the immediate problem of water scarcity. The project will also focus on building the capacity of institutions and people as an entry point for sustainable behavior change if accompanied by awareness about climate risks and water availability. This can be done through training, workshops, seminars, and other activities in schools and in public institutions especially schools and hospitals; and (ii) engagement of the private sector in the provision of services such as water supply, sanitation, and hygiene. The project will also focus on building technologies for water harvesting and optimization of water use such as water saving technologies and gadgets for both domestic and on-farm use. The project theory further postulates that building technical capacity at the level of the Hydrological Basin (HB) is critical to address water scarcity. This will enable people make more strategic decisions about water investments and add value to how public resources are utilized for water management. The project will also focus on building resilience in agriculture by addressing soil fertility, flood protection, soil conservation and drought management which will have an impact not only in the project area but also in other areas of the country.

The project will be implemented through a multi-stakeholder approach with the aim to bring about a strategic shift in the manner in which the Government plans and budgets for water infrastructure investments. The project will focus on building resilience at the level of the Hydrological Basin (HB) by bringing together stakeholders from different sectors such as agriculture, health, education, environment, etc.

## Investing in building the resilience of farming households is a promising and sustainable pathway based on the following: (i) enhanced knowledge about climate change risks and its impact on farming will encourage farmers to be more proactive in terms of change behavior and adaptation; (ii) seed and fertilizer can help them introduce adaptive practices such as using improved crop calendars, purchase new technologies and inputs for more efficient and productive use of water through Farming Field School (FFS); (iii) information communication technology can be used to disseminate climate information and provide actionable advice on how to adapt and adopt practices that are communicated through the extension services; (iv) women farmers who are often responsible for household food security, water management, vegetable gardening, feeding of livestock and are disproportionately impacted by climate change (UN: Women 2018) would be more willing to adopt and disseminate these practices and technologies if they were provided with the necessary support such as access to credit; and (v) a cadre of community-based women change agents can be key in reaching vulnerable women from households that are most affected by climate change.

## An underlying and core aspect of the theory of change of the current project is that as a result of differences in socially constructed gender roles and social status, women and men experience the impacts of climate change differently. Women are often responsible for household food security, water management, vegetable gardening, feeding of livestock and are disproportionately impacted by climate change (UN: Women 2018). Therefore, if interventions are to deliver sustainable benefits and so in an equitable way, they cannot afford to neglect these women who are the most vulnerable group. The project will focus on building resilience among them as this is critical for making the transition to climate-smart agriculture and meeting the food and nutrition security needs of an expanding population in an equitable and sustainable way (FAO: 2020). Therefore, the project will focus on building resilience at the level of farming households by providing women with the necessary support such as access to credit.

# The theory of change for scaling up the scope and impact of the project in a cost-effective and efficient manner is based on several key and well-tested assumptions that have proved the test of time:

- (i) the focus of the project on capacity building and technology transfer is expected to instill long-term behavioral changes among households, institutions, and communities;
- (ii) the replicability of the project to other areas is expected to be underpinned by the demonstration effect of the current users of the technologies and services provided in the pilot area; and
- (iii) the investment in the capacity building of local tiers of municipal government, civil society and the cadre of climate-wise women is expected to pay dividends in terms of the incorporation of climate change adaptation measures in their plans and strategies for implementation at the community level.

The investment in the curriculum of the academic and vocational institutions will be used for teaching and training to students and staff on climate change adaptation. The capacity building of local tiers of municipal government, civil society and build resilience.

## B.3 Project/programme description (max 2,000 words; approximately 4 pages)

26. The goal of the project is to increase climate-resilient sustainable development in the country to decrease vulnerability of rural areas to CC-induced water scarcity. The project is designed to help the country adapt to climate risks and is aligned with the country's Green Growth Plan (2017) which stresses the importance of shifting behavior and making efficient use of water resources, knowledge and information, and investment in renewable energy. The project is also aligned with other objectives of the Climate Change Policy (2015-2030), the National Adaptation Plan (2020) and to meet the objectives identified in the Jordan Water Sector Strategy (2016-2025). The project will focus on increasing water supply through storage of treated wastewater and rooftop rainwater harvesting and reducing household water consumption through the use of water-saving devices. To assess the project's benefits, it is necessary to quantify the reduction in groundwater overdraft in the Dead Sea Basin. Groundwater overdraft occurs when groundwater use exceeds the amount of natural recharge into an aquifer through rainfall, which leads to a decline in groundwater levels and can cause land subsidence.

27. According to the Jordanian Ministry of Water and Irrigation (http://on.jpwater.com/25K6C3T), the existing groundwater overdraft in the basin is 28.6 KMc/year. While this value is subject to uncertainty and project implementation will not be able to reduce it, the project will aim to quantify the reduction in available groundwater to benchmark the project's contribution to reducing groundwater overdraft. This value

In addition to the water balance presented in Table 1A, the table also presents the water obtained through water saving devices in households; the table presents the water balance for optimistic (top) and base (bottom) scenarios. As shown in Tables 1A/B, the project is expected to contribute to a 3 to 5% reduction in water consumption in households.

The project is expected to contribute to a reduction in groundwater overdraft from the current level of 3.4% to 0.31 MCM or 4.4%, to close the gap towards this goal and support the expansion of its impacts at the national level thanks to the extensive training and capacity development of communities, civil society organizations, and local authorities.

The project will have three interrelated components which will work synergistically and enhance the impact of project investments. The components are designed to tie with the key barriers identified above and are grouped into three components addressing climate change vulnerability via: water infrastructures, biodiversity and ecosystem services (BES) for enhanced livelihoods and food security.

- Component 1: Climate Resilient Water Systems for enhanced water security.
- Component 2: Climate Change resilience for Enhanced Livelihoods and Food Security.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 18 OF 86

## Component D: Scaling-up climate adaptation policy and across actors (institutions, private sector, civil society).

### 30. Component 1: Climate Resilient Water Systems for enhanced water security
This component has been designed to enhance water security at the farming community level in the Dead Sea Basin which has been severely affected by climate change. The project will focus on smallholder farmers who are currently struggling to cope with the described climate changes (e.g., increased temperatures, reduced rainfall and more frequent extreme weather events). These farmers have limited access to irrigation water and the overall draft from available resources, will make agriculture possible again as it once was due to lack of water farmers are currently growing marginal crops (e.g. barley for livestock) if not completely leaving their land in the cities.

#### 30.1. Climate Resilient Water Systems for enhanced water security and adaptation to mitigate climate change adverse impacts
Current sources of water supply are quite variable, limited and cause further water stress on the already stressed Dead Sea Basin. To break from this cycle and bring about a paradigm shift in how water is used and managed (Section D:2), the project will invest in proven water technologies that can be scaled up to meet the needs of smallholder farmers. The project will focus on rainwater harvesting structures (10–20 m²) and water saving devices for households and public buildings.

#### 30.1.1. Output 1:499 public buildings and 7,850 households will be fitted with the roof top water harvesting structures
Associated Activities include: the procurement of integrated water resilient systems, the construction of rainwater harvesting structures (10–20 m²) for public buildings and households, and the installation of water saving devices in public buildings. The roof-top water harvesting technology is simple, standardized for Jordan by the Uniform Plumbing Code of Jordan (UPCJ), and has been proven to be effective in other countries. It captures rainwater from roofs that fall on rooftops is collected into a dedicated tank instead of being dispersed and lost.

The size and type of rainwater harvesting structures will depend upon the roof area, the number of people living in the household, and the water demand for both domestic use and crops planted in the homestead gardens. The project considered that households would use about 23% of this water for drinking purposes and another 77% for crop irrigation.

Water saving devices include spray taps, faucet aerators, pressure reducing valves and low-flow shower heads with shut-off valves to be installed in homes. Cohesively, one of the selection criteria of beneficiary households is that they have a roof area greater than or equal to 10 m². The project will also consider other selection criteria specified in Annex page 56. This activity will address the shortage of water at the household level.

However, it should be noted that the rainwater harvesting structures are not designed for daily use and in project areas households are supplied with water once every week or two. Therefore, this activity aims to provide a solution to the problem of water scarcity by providing an alternative source of water supply for both domestic use[1] and for crops planted in the homestead gardens that do not require constant irrigation and that are drought tolerant. To this end, the project considered that households would use about 23% of the rainwater harvested on roofs and another 77% for crop irrigation.

From the simulations made by the project, the following pattern for a 20 m² system that included irrigation

32. Furthermore, households will also have access to the facilities designed to increase water productivity: adopt new irrigation techniques and use of drought-resistant crops that will minimize water requirements both in house and outside (i.e., home garden) [Component 2]. Therefore, the provision of rainwater harvesting systems is not planned to substitute rainfed irrigation in open fields but to optimize the use of potential rainfall for household consumption.

33. The project will support the installation of rainwater harvesting systems from rooftops in rural areas (Annex 2, pages 53-95). The system requires a rooftop to collect rainwater that will be transferred by gravity into a tank located underground or above ground depending on soil type, foundations and space availability. Figure 6 illustrates the main elements of the system.

**Page 25**
---

34\. Rainwater harvesting systems will also be installed in selected public buildings such as schools, mosques, municipalities for dissemination and awareness on the technology at the local community level and to also help address water scarcity in public buildings. Details of the criteria to determine the technical feasibility have been detailed in Annex 2, pages 52-967.

35\. The type of tank will be determined by the site characteristics/attributes (e.g. roof area, soil type and hydrogeological conditions) as per the Technical Guidelines for Rainwater Harvesting Systems (Annex 2, pages 56 and 58) and will serve to guide the bidding package for the service provider. These and the results of the technical feasibility assessment will be used by the project team in collaboration with the relevant local authorities and NGOs and CBOs will determine the final design of the RHS’s tank. The final design will necessarily be in line with environmental and social safeguards, as per the Annex 6 “Environmental and Social Management Framework” (ESMF) and the Technical Guidelines for Rainwater Harvesting Systems (Annex 2). The ESMF (including, for example, inappropriate site selection or deployment of inappropriate or ineffective conservation technologies) will be used to assess the potential environmental impacts and social risks as well as the reported specification follows the standards reported by the USAID, 2017 Rainwater Harvesting Systems Technical Guidelines. The design of the tank will also be in line with the recommendations made by the Jordan National Building Council’s technical committee in 2015 (reference attached). The design of the tank will be based on a calculation that takes into account rainfall volume, roof size, number of people per household and runoff coefficient. A detailed description of sources and calculation is reported in Appendix 1 to Annex 2, pages 46-948.

36\. A service provider will be competitively selected among NGOs and CBOs for the identification of the households and to develop a behavior change strategy for more efficient water use in homes and in the public buildings. The project team will monitor the implementation of the RHS and provide technical assistance to beneficiaries and MWI project staff will monitor the execution of works. Each installed unit will be georeferenced and available in the project Atlas and tracked for performance assessment (Annex 2, page 58). The project team will also monitor the impact of the RHS on water conservation activities. One of the criteria for the selection of the service provider will be their previous experience in implementing similar projects. The project team will also assess the potential water savings activities at the school level. The awareness among children is expected to generate additional benefits as young people are being recognized as the champions or change agents who will promote climate change adaptation and mitigation practices within the school and broader geographic area in the Governorates based on technical feasibility and potential for using the water for homestead gardens.

**D**

### The investment will be accompanied by behavioral change, awareness and orientation activities on water conservation at the household level, for municipal staff of public buildings and children and teachers in schools. These activities are to be carried out through project activities such as demonstrations, training, update and diffusion of water saving devices in households and public buildings. Water-saving devices include spray taps, faucet aerators, pressure reducing valves and low-flow shower heads with shut-off valves (attached to the tap). The use of these devices is expected to reduce water consumption by 30% and save 30% of water used in buildings (USAID 2005). The government – often with support from international donors – has been promoting the use of such devices for many years. These devices are now aligned with existing policies (USAID 2018) (Annex A page 56–57). Therefore, the project will present in schools and other public buildings the advantages of identified devices that will allow families to save water. The technical assistance provided by the project (e.g., installation of low-flow shower heads with shut-off valves) will not require technical expertise to be installed and they do not require maintenance. Children and teachers will be trained on how to use these devices, and their parents will be encouraged through engagement to further enhance the efficiency with which the water is used at the domestic level given that almost half the water is used for domestic purposes in the country. The activity will be introduced in a phased manner (Annex A page 56–57).**

### The project will also provide training and awareness raising activities on rainwater harvesting systems to households, women-headed families, refugees and poor households. These activities are based on beneficiaries' readiness and capacity to receive the rainwater harvesting system. Beneficiaries will be identified through a participatory process (phase i/ii) and will be selected by HHs themselves. The project is going that through specific awareness and training events for identifying feasibility and optimal operation and maintenance of rainwater harvesting systems, HHs will be able to make informed decisions on whether they have in ensuring water security (phase iii). 17.6% of the output's budget will be allocated to raising awareness and education activities. A team of specialists will be in charge of planning, providing technical assistance (27 staff) and implementing these activities (30 staff). The total number of staff is 57 people out of the output’s budget. The team of specialists will include experts from the ministry of water and irrigation, experts from USAID, and local experts with experience in rainwater harvesting systems implementation in Jordan. While planning will be fully in charge of specialists of the Executive Entity, oversight and monitoring will be done in collaboration with the Ministry of Water and Irrigation. Technical assistance will be provided by project staff and other technical experts as needed.

### The households that benefit from the rainwater harvesting system will be expected to contribute part of the costs based on criteria that favours women-headed households, refugees and poor households; those referred to as high priority (HH). HHs will be responsible for maintaining their systems and using the water for homemade gardens for increased food security. While men and women will both be consulted on designing water outlets, special care will be taken to involve women as the primary users of domestic water in the household. The project will provide training sessions on rainwater harvesting systems. Public buildings will be based on water use in the buildings such as schools, mosques, hospitals and the willingness to participate in the project. HHs will be provided with information about the benefits of rainwater harvesting and participating in orientation sessions on more efficient water use. These will be directly paid by the HH to the seller of these gadgets. The amount will not enter project books but the record of who has installed these (offer verification during the feasibility assessment, and the cost will be entered in the M&E system.

* A team of technical specialists comprising a water engineer and an NRE specialist will provide guidance to HHs and oversight of service providers on all technical aspects of rainwater harvesting systems.

### Output 3.1.2: Reuse of reclaimed water from 3 Waste Waters Plants is optimized (Infrastructure at the wastewater treatment site in project areas is inadequate and does not have the capacity to store reclaimed water[1]) for use in the dry season and/or for supplementary irrigation to at least 968[2] climate vulnerable farmers in Malawi, Karak, and Zambia.

The proposed activity will address this issue by building infrastructure that will allow the storage of reclaimed water. The reclaimed water will be stored during the winter months and distributed to farmers during the dry season when water demand is at its highest. Water distribution during the dry season will be managed by farmers and their communities through a community-based irrigation system (CBIS). This will ensure that water is available when needed for farmers mitigating the adverse impacts of climate change (e.g., droughts, floods) on food security.

The project will not impose any penalties or restrictions on the use of reclaimed water for fodder irrigation[3] that still not penalize beneficiaries nor limit the food security potential of the country. As reported in Annex 3, the economic and financial analysis demonstrate the viability of this activity. The project will also ensure that the quality of the reclaimed water is safe to be used by farmers irrigated with reclaimed water. Forage crops meet less than 25% of livestock feed requirements and the rest must be imported from neighboring countries at high cost. This will reduce the importation costs, and it has been estimated that the high cost of freshwater for vegetables joined with volatile market prices increases the investment in irrigation infrastructure to a level that is not affordable by smallholder farmers (see Annex 19). The project will not impose nor push for any particular crop. The alfalfa is reported in the documents and analyzed in Annex 19 as it appears to be the favorite crop for farmers that can access irrigation water.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 22 OF 86

## Infrastructure will be built to maximize the use of the reclaimed water (Annex 13; see MWR 2014). The project will assist the MWI and the MoA in building capacity of Water User Associations (WUAs) among farmers that are or will be connected to reclaimed water sources. WUAs will be supported in affording the necessary infrastructure for the development, operation, maintenance, and management of the system.

The project will also support WUAs in ensuring transparency and rational water sharing rights among farmers and other users, rules and applications forms to allow possible enlargements of the farm network for use by additional subscribers.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2 | PAGE 23 OF 86

## Landscape Investment Plans

### Component 2: Climate change resilience for enhanced livelihoods and food security.

This component has three outputs:
- (i) Enhanced capacity of households to deal with climate change;
- (ii) strengthening of a system of extension for wider dissemination through use of Information Communication Technology (ICT);
- (iii) establishing a cadre of climate-wise women for climate adaptation. The expected outcome from this component is enhanced capacity of households to deal with climate change.

#### Output 2.1.1: By year seven, 6,000 women and men farmers will be trained in climate resilient production systems and practices.

- **Sub-component 1:** By year six, 3,500 women and men farmers will have been trained.
- **Sub-component 2:** By year seven, 2,200 will be women farmers. The first sub-component is intended to strengthen the capacity of farming households to adapt to climate change and build their resilience. The project will help people understand what they need to do in order to manage changes that are happening or likely to happen in response to changing climate. The project will focus on crops relevant for the area such as barley, olives, vines, etc., and vegetables such as tomatoes, peppers, eggplants, etc.

#### Focus Areas (FFS)

- **Area 1:** Barley
- **Area 2:** Olives
- **Area 3:** Vineyards
- **Area 4:** Vegetables

### Main Climate Change Adaptation Benefits from the project and priority adaptation measures (PAMs)

- Reduced water use by plants due to temperature increase.
- Increased evaporation caused by higher temperatures. This will potentially allow for increased productivity of crops in the area.

**Page 31**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 24 OF 86

## Water harvesting infrastructures (on farm level)

### Conservation Agriculture (CA) practices (e.g., crop rotation and extensive fallow)
- Increase water availability at the field level and increase soil moisture
- Increase soil moisture, reduce evaporation losses, and enhance the soils' physical properties
- Increase yields of cereal crops producers (up to 20% increase in yield per hectare for wheat and barley grains)

### Protect and semi-protective crop residues (on farm level)
- All farmers with home gardens and irrigated
- Increase productivity by water retention, increase soil moisture content in limited areas

### Fertilization of crops (using locally available organic fertilizers)
- All farmers with home gardens
- Increase productivity per unit of water and more stable income for households.

## Container and soiless cultivation (greenhouses and hydroponics)

### Agroforestry and soil conservation practices (seed pruning, mulching, intercropping, addition of organic matter, root and soil mulching, etc.)
- All farmers with home gardens
- Increase productivity per unit of water and more stable income for households.

## Irrigation and cultivation with recycled water

### Rainfed farming to better-managed rainfed systems
- All fodder producers
- Increase productivity by water retention in limited areas

### CAMI (1) Improving soil water storage to increase water availability at the field level and increase soil moisture content.
- Maximizing infiltration of rainfall; increasing surface runoff; improving soil structure and surface roughness; increasing water holding capacity, and reducing evaporation losses through supplementary irrigation from harvested rainwater or groundwater.
- Optimization of planting and harvesting.

### CCAM (Farmers will be able to cope with the effects of climate change using improved crop management practices.)
- Farmers will be able to produce more food per unit of water and more stable income for households.

## 45. The project will use the Farmer Field School (FFS) approach which is conducted on the farmer's field over a period of several months.
The MoA staff (90% are female, 10% are male employees) will be trained as FFS Facilitators and trained for the purpose. The approach to FFS will focus on climate adaptive aspects of both crops and livestock production. It is expected that the project will increase the adoption rates of climate change adaptation practices by farmers in the area, increase their productivity rates and sustained behaviour change.
Experience of projects like the IFAD financed Rural Economic Growth and Agroforestry Project (REGAP) has shown that FFS can be an effective tool for training farmers and workshops to scale up tested techniques and practices. The project will design and organize separate FFS for women (at least 70%) by identifying topics of interest to them and also encourage women to participate in the FFS sessions.
The project will provide training on climate change adaptation technologies, including the practice and technology to others in the area. The Lead farmers will be given additional incentives and inputs to act as role models for other farmers in the area. The project will also organize a series of field days and outreach of the adaptation technologies and practices over time.
22% of total education budget per farmer in Action 2.1.16 (FFS implementation) is expected to provide the necessary supplies and inputs. The private


---
**Page 32**
---

sector inputs and technologies will also be engaged throughout the process and will be invited to demonstrate their inputs and technologies as they will be the ones expected to provide the improved technologies and inputs to farmers on a sustained basis based on the market potential. There are a range of activities that will be undertaken in order to achieve this objective.

### 45. The set of activities identified to achieve this output include design of appropriate modules for Climate Smart FFS. Six modules will be developed by specialists in the first year and fourth year in collaboration with the Ministry for implementation. These experts must be paired up to develop these training modules. Technical assistance will be procured for the purpose of developing NGOs and private firms that will take responsibility for the FFS and will help develop work plans, training modules in climate change adaptation and mitigation technologies, and other related activities such as field days. The component will be implemented by the Extension Agents of the Ministry of Agriculture. The Extension Agents will be responsible for implementing the Climate Smart FFS and disseminating these technologies. The activities will be undertaken in coordination with the National Agriculture Research Centre (NARC) as a key implementing partner, which has four research centres in the selected Governorates and also provides technical assistance to farmers through its extension services and other related projects.

### 46. Technical Assistance (TA) will be recruited in the first year and support the project throughout its implementation period. TA will be responsible for providing training modules, training materials, and TA or university to take full responsibility for developing work plans, training modules and coordinating with MoA and NARC for execution by staff and identifying and organizing the venues, inputs and other requirements for the field days. The TA will also provide technical assistance in the implementation of the FFS and organize it in a manner which is relevant for women and encourages their participation.

### 47. Conducting the FFS would involve a series of preparatory activities which will be undertaken by the project including Training a team of Master Trainers/Field Agents: A team of 40 Master Trainers or Field Agents (MTs) will be recruited from among the Extension Agents in the selected Governorates and in the Dead Sea Basin and Nahr. They will be provided 30 days training in the technology to be disseminated, including climate change adaptation and mitigation technologies. At least one refresher training will be held as required. The Extension Agents of the MoA will have the main responsibility for implementing the Climate Smart FFS and disseminating these technologies based on the following criteria: (i) Participants who have been impacted by climate change; (ii) relevance of the training for them; (iii) commitment to attend the designated sessions; (iv) commitment to maintain records on what they learn from the training, and (v) willingness to disseminate the learning to other farmers. At least 70 FFS will be organized exclusively for women in the Dead Sea Basin and Nahr Governorates. The MTs will be selected based on their willingness to disseminate the learning to others. Women will also be encouraged to participate in the main FFS where appropriate. The project will refine the existing application used to track climate resilience adaptation disaggregated by sex, the Ministry of Agriculture will provide technical assistance for this purpose. This tool is a database that tracks adoption rates over time and reports the impact of the technologies and practices disseminated.

### 48. The MTs will be provided with training materials including handouts, flip charts, posters, etc., which will be used to train farmers on climate change adaptation and mitigation technologies. The MTs will also be trained in using the database that tracks adoption rates over time and reports the impact of the technologies and practices disseminated.

#### 43. The project will finance 270 FFS with a total of 4,500 participants. The FFS will be conducted by the Agriculture Extension Officers of the MoA. It is expected that on average there will be 17 farmers per group.

The project will organize 70 FFS exclusively for women with a minimum participation of 1200 women. Apart from the above, the project will also conduct 30 FFS exclusively for men with a minimum participation of 600 men. The FFS will be conducted by Extension Agents and NARC staff. The staff time of the Government staff will be the cost of their salary and transport to the field. The cost of food and accommodation during the training, as well as food in the field will be paid for the project. The project will provide inputs for the FFS (seed, wicking beds, grow bags, saplings, low-cost material for greenhouses, etc.) as well as the technologies that are being disseminated. The following four components will be implemented on Farmer Fields and where required, the four NARC research centres will be used.

#### 43a. NARC has tested a host of climate adaptive technologies which it will demonstrate and disseminate here in this project. The modes operated by us are field days, workshops and exchange visits. It is expected that the project will have a total of 1200 participants from the four NARC research centres and other areas in Amuria to disseminate these technologies. It is expected that 6,000 people will benefit from these field days. The private sector has also been invited to participate in this project by providing their products for demonstrations. Given the COVID-19 pandemic and its disruption of the supply lines, it will be an extremely challenging task to ensure that all the required inputs are available on time. NARC is currently working on establishing home-based vegetable gardens using water-efficient technologies. NARC will contribute its staff time and use of its facilities for the purpose. The project will only finance the out-of pocket costs for travel and accommodation during these field days.

The project will also provide a platform to introduce their products, location, contact numbers and prices for ensuring sustainable supply lines and promote a market-based approach.

#### 50. An independent impact assessment will be undertaken by a third party to assess the quality of the demonstrations, participants feedback, adoption rates and the extent to which participation in the FFS and field days has helped in introducing to the farming households adaptive technologies and practices and its contribution towards climate change resilience. A high-quality report will be produced to record the experience and draw lessons from it for the future.

#### 51. Output 2.1.3: At least 30% of the farming households in the country or 30,000 farming households are reached through the e-extension system or Information Communication Technology for Climate Change Adaptation (ICT4CA) which is being developed by NARC and MoA. The project will strengthen and consolidate the efforts of the MoA (DG-External Relations and NARC) and MiIE in disseminating information on climate change adaptation using the e-extension system and ICT4CA as well as other forms of ICT4CA which they have put in place (Annex 2, para 174). NARC is also investing in developing a system for propagating tested research through the use of smart applications. Tailored gender-responsive extension services will be provided to farmers using this system. The information on climate change adaptation will be broadcast through this mechanism. This is all the more important given the limited outreach of the traditional extension services and the fact that the majority of the rural population in Uganda does not have access to mobile phones. This approach can also prove valuable in case of any future travel restrictions. The project will undertake the following specific activities under this sub-component.

Developing climate IT solutions for smart devices: The project will assist in developing and testing climate information services that are used by MoA, NARC and MiIE for disseminating information on climate change adaptation. ### 54. Output 2.13: Establishing a cadre of 400 climate wise women change agents for climate adaptation:

The third sub-component will train a cadre of women as change agents. Women in rural areas are disproportionately affected by climate change with unequal access to resources and assets, barriers to decision-making and limited access to information on climate change. Empowering rural women as agents of change – leaders, practitioners, educators and influencers in climate change adaptation and mitigation. Empowering rural women as agents of change for climate adaptation has been identified in the literature as critical to achieving sustainable development goals.

In collaboration with the Ministry of Women’s Affairs, the project will create a cadre of 400 young women as agents of change for climate adaptive practices from the rural areas in the four target governorates. These young women will


---
**Page 35**
---

be advanced as repositories of knowledge and technical guidance and support on climate change adaptation, anchored in rural communities. The women will be trained and certified through a customized sixteen-week course delivered over the course of a year in state-of-the-art techniques for climate adaptive agriculture and agroforestry. They will also receive training in advocacy skills to empower them as community advocates. The presence of these young women in the rural communities will bring practical knowledge and support to their families, while also empowering them with new skills that can be used by themselves and youth, the benefits of project interventions. At the national level, it will highlight women’s role as change agents in climate change in Jordan and provide the government with informed, community-based information on how best to address climate change.

50. The project will undertake a set of activities to establish the cadre of women agents that will include procuring technical assistance for the purpose to design a proper course and training manual for the purpose. The course content will include modules on irrigation technology, rainwater harvesting and soil management techniques, agroforestry, climate adaptive agriculture and agroforestry technologies, budgeting and business plan development. The course will be developed in English and Arabic. A Memorandum of Understanding will be signed with a Jordanian university to provide the training service. The training will be delivered by Master Trainers for Climate Adaptation (MTCA) trained under the Adaptation, will be delivered over 6 months to 8 young women agronomists to train them as Master Trainers for Climate Adaptation (MTCA). The MTCA will be trained in collaboration with the Ministry of Agriculture through multiple channels such as Universities, Colleges, newspapers, social media and Civil Society Organization representatives. The training will be conducted by a team of experts from the MoA and other partners. An agreement signed between the young women agronomists and the MoA specifying the obligation to complete the course and deliver the climate adaptation training to a specified number of young women in their communities will be signed.

* Resident in Taifan, Maada, Madaka and Karak (2 from each Governorate)
* Excellent interpersonal and communication skills
* Willing to travel

## Output 2.14 By year 7, 15,000 Persons sensitized for climate adaptive measures.

The trained women agents will be required to hold community dialogues, underline households visits highlighting the importance of integrating climate change adaptation into their daily lives and household activities. The change through the key role they play in the domestic and agriculture sectors and in their role as mothers and caregivers. The Climate Women will also host dialogues with groups of women, men and youth in the communities to provide them with information on climate change and its impacts on their livelihoods and how they can adapt to it. They will be advised on ways to improve their lives and undertake household visits to advise on adaptation measures at the domestic and farm level. These visits will include advice on water harvesting techniques, use of solar energy, construction of small greenhouses, drip irrigation systems, production and use of organic goods etc., at the homestead and farm level. It is expected that 100 women from each Government and at least one woman from each community will receive an activity-based session. The Climate Wise Women will also use social media to lead dialogues on climate change especially with young people and introduce them to modern climate adaptive agriculture.

### Organizing multi-stakeholder climate-wise women forums

The Climate Wise Women Forums will be organized in Year 4,5 and 6 of the project. These events will serve to highlight the role of women as change agents; identify achievements and challenges for climate adaptation at the community level for women farmers and youth. The forums will also provide a platform for women farmers and youth to address climate change. These events will be highly interactive with modern techniques like World Cafe sessions, group discussions, etc., and will use tools such as the Climate Wise Women’s Decision Making Systems Tool used to develop a gender-sensitive vision for Climate Change. The participants will be the climate wise women from the target communities, young men and women from target communities, Government officials from the MOA and MOE at the district level, Agriculture and Environment departments at the governorate level and relevant Civil Society Organizations.

# 61. Component 3: Scaling-up climate adaptation policy and across actors (institutions, private sector, civil society)

This component will be instrumental to scale up impacts, ensure long term sustainability of investments in climate change adaptation, and promote full and equitable ownership of the identified transformational path. As detailed in Annex D (pages 35-37) and (D1) the main factors that contribute to this are: (i) the institutional lack of coherence between the national strategies and the sectoral agricultural/municipal/irrigation water subsectors do not play in favor of optimization and shift to improved technologies across the country; (ii) the limited extension services provided by the government for fodder production and water subsidies that do not play in favor of optimization and shift to improved technologies across the country; and (iii) the lack of institutional capacity and skills among farmers and other stakeholders for their livelihood, very limited action is taken to ensure the adequate technology and knowledge transfer to farming communities due to limited and weak extension services. The combination of these factors have led to inefficient use of resources and low productivity in the agricultural sector.

## 62. Therefore, the main outcome of this component will be to mainstream gender sensitive adaptive tools and practices to adapt to water scarcity in the national policy and educational framework. This will ensure that the institutional capacity and skills among farmers and other stakeholders is secure by addressing identified policy bottlenecks with stakeholders and upgrading the capacities of institutions (government, civil society organizations and private sector) that constitute the front line of adaptation in rural areas.

The 3 outcomes that will help to mainstream gender sensitive adaptive tools and practices as part of climate change adaptation strategies:

### Output 3.1.1: Specific policy instruments for scaling up climate adaptation are identified and reforms instituted

The project will support governments, institutions and stakeholders to ensure that the factors inhibiting adaptation of the agriculture and water sectors to climate change in Jordan are addressed. Therefore, based on lessons learned, the project will support partnering institutions and the national climate change committee (NCCC) with specific policy instruments for scaling up climate adaptation.

Specifically, the project intends to encourage the following:

- Adoption of drought tolerant wheat varieties and other crops that are more resilient to water scarcity.
- Development of a framework for providing financial incentives/subsidies based on adoption of climate smart field and water management practices (e.g. drip irrigation) in order to promote the use of water efficient technologies, obtain premium water and agriculture subsidies to progressively shift from production based subsidies/incentives to climate adaptive incentives/subsidies based on adoption of climate smart field and water management practices.
- Development of a framework for providing financial incentives/subsidies based on adoption of climate smart field and water management practices (e.g. drip irrigation) in order to promote the use of water efficient technologies, obtain premium water and agriculture subsidies to progressively shift from production based subsidies/incentives to climate adaptive incentives/subsidies based on adoption of climate smart field and water management practices.
