### Training for teachers and professors to enable the teaching and practice of the new curriculum

The project will work with the ministry of Education and universities to develop an updated climate change curriculum for teachers/students/teachers/trainers/counselors. The course will be to mainstream gender-sensitive climate change adaptation practices and technologies among teachers, instructors, and students. This will include development of training materials, preparation and distribution of learning materials, execution of the training modules, monitoring and evaluation of the trainings.

#### 68. Output 3.1 Capacity building of host 6448 persons from the private sector, the civil society and local government (20 governors, 18 provinces, 22 municipalities) engaged in climate change adaptation practices

In line with the Paris agreement recommendation “governments, development agencies, and other stakeholders should support countries to mainstream gender-sensitive approaches into their national strategies for adapting to climate risks (...) and concerted capacity-building, particularly at the local level, is needed to move from improved information to better decisions” the project will work with local institutions to ensure that proposed climate change adaptation practices and technologies are appropriate, relevant, and have clear benefits and that is ready to support communities with a local planning approach that is proactive and tailored to their needs. This will be done through the development of a joint strategic planning process of both civil society organizations and the private sector. Both play a critical role in reducing the adaptation deficit of target areas and in transferring practices and technologies as also recommended by the Paris agreement.

#### 70. Local engagement and dissemination process

The project will work with a range of civil society organizations, project area actors as well as national level and the local media to develop the appropriate approaches and actions to engage communities in project areas and develop a local engagement plan for mainstreaming gender-sensitive climate change adaptation practices and technologies. This will provide information on the source of advice and additional information and communication materials. This will involve Coordination and gender inclusive consultation with local NGOs, CBOs and other representation of the private sector. The project will also work to ensure that all stakeholders are involved in the preparation of information/training/knowledge sharing modules, execution of the local engagement plan and monitoring and evaluation.

#### 71. Technical assistance to enhance local administration’s and private sector actors’ capacities

The project will support local governments and private sector actors in developing and implementing gender-sensitive climate change adaptation practices and technologies. This includes training for plumbers, electricians, including women technicians) in adopting gender-sensitive tools and practices. The project will also provide technical advice and guidance to local governments and administrations. This will include provision of training and technical assistance on how to comply with existing policy frameworks and practices as well as address the limitation and bottlenecks preventing the adoption of gender-sensitive climate change adaptation practices and technologies within the project. Gender-inclusive Consultation will be held with institutions (local) and the private sector and civil society organizations in order to ensure that all stakeholders are involved in the preparation of the local engagement plan for mainstreaming gender-sensitive climate change adaptation practices and technologies. The private sector will be constantly involved in project activities to ensure technology transfer and to stimulate the adoption of gender-sensitive climate change adaptation practices and Technical assistance and training to civil society organizations: The project recognizes the potential of the civil society organizations and their role in supporting beneficiaries in overcoming the adaptation deficit. Nonetheless, the knowledge and technical skills of Jordanian civil society organizations are limited. Therefore, the project will provide them with technical assistance and further support before their contribution can effectively and efficiently deployed in assisting rural communities to adapt to climate change.

The project will focus on three main components: (1) Technical assistance and training and capacity development process via (a) Dedicated trainings, workshops and conferences; (b) Providing technical assistance through dedicated technical advisors; (c) Allowing replication and scaling up of FFS approaches and introduced practices[9]; (d) Climate adaptive technologies and practices in agriculture. In these regards the project will replicate for civil society organizations the same process that was used to train the staff of MoA, and the training of the staff of the MoA. The project will work primarily with those civil society organizations (max 4 per governorate) which have a strong presence in rural areas and are active in water and agriculture sectors.

The project will link participants with other projects to enhance their knowledge on climate change adaptation and mitigation, and we would like to include them in the national CCA plan and capacity development action plan to ensure full mainstreaming of CCA and water saving practices across the local civil society.

B.4 Implementation arrangements (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagrams)

72. FAD will serve both as GCF Accredited Entity responsible for supervising the BRCCJ project and as Executive Entity for project execution. The independence of the two roles will be guaranteed by D establishing two separate functions as follows:

73. FAD as Accredited Entity: FAD's supervisory role will be attributed to the FAO Regional Office for New Zealand and the Pacific (ROPNZP) in Suva, Fiji, and to the FAO Country Bureau (CB), located in Rome, Italy and other technical divisions as required. In its role as the AE, FAD will undertake the following responsibilities: (i) ensuring that the project is implemented according to the approved supervision throughout project implementation; (ii) securing funds are effectively managed to deliver results and achieve objectives; (iii) ensure the quality of project monitoring, as well as the timeliness and quality of reporting on project progress; (iv) ensuring that the project complies with all applicable laws and regulations in accordance with the detailed provisions outlined in the Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA) between FAD and GCF. The LTO will be responsible for providing technical support to FAD in its supervisory role, including technical responsibility for project execution. The role of the LTO is central to FAO's comparative advantage for projects and to separate the functions of FAD in its role as Accredited Entity and as Executive Entity. The LTO will be responsible for ensuring that the project complies with all applicable laws and regulations and functions. The LTO will be responsible and accountable for providing or obtaining technical clearance of project documents, including but not limited to project proposals, project implementation plans, and mission reports. Missions may call other experts to participate and advice and will provide technical backstopping to the Project Team to ensure the delivery of quality technical outputs.

74. FAD Jordan will act as the budget holder (BiH) for the project for funds from the GCF and assume the role of Executive Entity for the project, in accordance with the PMU. The diagram on flow of funds is given below. The funds will be disbursed by GCF based on Annual Work Plans to FAO Headquarters or other offices as required directly to FAD Jordan. The GCF funds will be transferred from FAO to UNDP under a subsidiary agreement (UNA) in an UN Agreement. The overall control of the disbursement will follow FAD rules and regulations.

75. FAO as Executing Entity: project delivery functions will be undertaken by FAO, including providing all fundamental services for the management of the envisaged activities. More specifically, following the principles to ensure the highest level of ownership and sustainability of the project investment at country level (i.e., within local institutions), FAD's role in the BRCCJ project will be limited to the provision of quality assurance and supervision functions. The LTO will provide technical support for the project, including replicability, and to ensure compliance with UNDP, MoF, MWL, MoA as co-financers/executing entities. FAO will also provide a range of services in line with its comparative advantage, including GCF supervised consultants and service providers. FAO will mandate as a global stakeholder in the field of agriculture, water resources, climate change, and food security, to ensure that the project is implemented and monitored, providing technical assistance and advice, and assurance. UNDP will be responsible for implementing activities related to roof-top water harvesting at the household level and the preparation of the Local Action Plan (LAP) in accordance with the GCF guidelines. The LTO and FAD Jordan have already identified as part of the contribution to the project and will be considered co-executing entities. UNDP and all three Ministries have provided letters confirming their contribution for co-financing (Annex 13).

76. UNDP as an Executing Entity: The project will be supported by the Environment, Climate Change and Disaster Risk Management Division of the United Nations Country Team in Fiji, which is supported by the Inclusive Growth and Sustainable Livelihoods team and will benefit from their experience of working with local communities in the project area. The Operations team at UNDP will facilitate the procurement of

goods and services during execution for the activities being executed by UNDP. The risks associated with the proposed interventions and determine the appropriate type and level of social and environmental safeguards, as well as any other measures required by law. UNDP will also work according to national laws and guidelines. UNDP will closely coordinate project execution with FAO at three different levels:

#### 77. The project will draw on the comparative advantages of FAO and UNDP in Jordan and has established strong partnerships in keeping with their strengths and experience. FAO has been involved in supporting activities in the agriculture sector including climate resilient agriculture and water management, as well as in the development of a national strategy for sustainable land management. FAO’s agricultural production and has focused on capacity development for intensifying Climate Smart Agriculture. FAO has also piloted conjunctive use of groundwater and captured surface water to ensure reliability of water supply in rural areas. UNDP has been involved in supporting the Jordan Water Sector Strategy, empowering local beneficiaries for the operation and maintenance in pilot areas. UNDP has a strong in-country presence and has established partnerships with key stakeholders.

#### 78. UNDP is responsible for working with the MoE and has supported a number of environmental policy reform actions in Jordan, notably in the climate change sector. It assisted with the development of the Integrated Investment Framework for Sustainable Land Management (2013), formulation of National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA) and National Adaptation Plan (NAP), Nationally Appropriate Mitigation Actions (NAMA), a plan for the phased introduction of low-emission technologies and energy efficiency measures, and the development of Jordan’s Climate Change Strategy. UNDP supported the Government of Jordan (GoJ) in developing the pioneering National Policy on Climate Change (2013) which formed the backbone for the Climate Change agenda in the country. Moreover, UNDP has been supporting GoJ to mainstream climate change into national sectoral policies and strategies. UNDP is also working with the MoE to support Jordan’s efforts towards climate resilience in mainstreaming and integration of climate change consideration into national and sectoral development plans. The project will leverage these existing partnerships by engaging key stakeholders in accordance with international best practices to enhance sustainable water harvesting solutions as a means to adapt to climate change impacts for local communities in different governorates in the country. The expected outcomes are that the project will contribute towards addressing the most pressing water-related issues. It, therefore, intended to leverage the existing partnerships with different partners including private sector and civil society organizations to ensure that the project is implemented effectively within the targeted governorates by the project. UNDP’s support with the Goal Waters Programme has been working to strengthen the Drought Governance system in the country including the basin targeted by the project.

#### 79. Ministry of Water and Irrigation (MoW): is the official body responsible for the overall monitoring of the water sector, water supply and wastewater system and the related projects, planning and management, the formulation of national water strategies and policies, research and development, information systems


---
**Page 43**
---

and procurement of financial resources. Its role also includes the provision of centralized water-related data,
standardization and codification of data.

80. MWI was established in 1988 by law issued by the executive branch of the Government under the Jordanian Constitution. The establishment of MWI was in response to Jordan's recognition of the need for a more efficient and effective management of its water resources. MWI is responsible for the implementation
by several donor organization projects that have assisted in the development of water policy and water
management plans at all levels of government. Some of these donors include: The World Bank, the United
States Agency for International Development (USAID), the European Union, the Swiss Agency for
Cooperation and Development (SDA), and the German Federal Ministry for Economic Cooperation and
Development (BMZ). MWI is also responsible to the Minister of Water and Irrigation with oversight
responsibility. The MWI Authority of Jordan (WAJ) and the Jordan Valley Authority (JVA) (Ministry Law No.82 of 1992).

81. MWI embraces the two most important issues dealing with water in Jordan: WAJ in charge of water
& sewerage, and JVA in charge of irrigation & water resources development of the Jordan Rift Valley,
including water development and distribution of irrigation. This relative position with respect to WAJ &
JVA allows for a more integrated approach to water management in Jordan. In addition to its Water Management System, MWI has become a leader in the region that uses GIS-based digital tools for Water Master Planning
activities, offering the framework, databases and tools necessary to manage water data and providing water
management services to other countries in the region. The Water Master Plan (WMP) is a comprehensive,
integrated approach to water resources management which produces essential water sector information products including the Water Master Plan.

82. Ministry of Agriculture (MoA): was established during the era of the East of Jordan in the formation
of the Department of Agriculture in 1946, and became an independent ministry within the agricultural sector since the beginning of the establishment of the emirate. The organization and management of the Ministry is Regulation No. 82 of 2004 and its amendments issued in accordance with Article 120 of the Constitution
and Law No. 35.

83. MoA is responsible for the agricultural sector, promoting self-sufficiency and rural development and linking the production to the requirements of the markets inside and outside Jordan. It also aims to promote plant production, control pests, preserve forests and pastures, and also regulate the establishment of livestock farms and animal husbandry. The Ministry is also responsible for the protection of animals against
disease and health and veterinary quarantine, as well as the role of the Ministry in the protection of wild birds and wild animals.

84. MoA consists of thirty-eight directorsates and a central unit, including two directorates linked to the
Minister, the directorate of the Minister's office and Directorate of Information, Communication and
Parliamentary Affairs, Internal Control Unit, and Agricultural Risk Management Fund; six Assistance Secretariats; three Directorates General; and twelve governorate agriculture directorates. In addition,
the Secretary General's office is directly linked to the Secretary General in addition to twelve governorate
agriculture directorates and thirty directorates of agriculture in the governorate brigades.

85. Ministry of Environment (MoE): established in 2003, is the main ministry responsible for climate change issues. MoE was created by Law No.14/2003 on 27th April 2003 and replaced Law No. 96/2014,
in the institutional role of coordinating and developing all activities related to UNFCCC. MoE oversees the policy and legal framework for climate change in Jordan, including the development of national policies and strategies.
Especially for the CCO, it is clear to reach out to stakeholders to develop actions for climate response, and
to incorporate the resulting policies and executive decision-making. It has become the focal point for
international climate treaties, including the UNFCCC, and the development of the National Communication

### Project Steering Committee and Technical Oversight

At the level of strategic guidance and oversight, the project will use the National Climate Change Council (NCCC) as its Steering Committee.

87. **Project Management Structure**: FAD will establish a Project Management Unit (PMU) nested in the Ministry Environment which will provide regular reports and performance updates to the MoE. The PMU will be responsible for overall planning and coordination, developing annual work plans and budgets, day-to-day project management, provide technical backstopping, financial management and undertake procurement of goods and services as required.

The Technical Working Group (TWG) is a multi-stakeholder group that includes representatives from the Hashemite Kingdom University (HKU), the Jordan University of Science and Technology (JUST) and the National Agricultural Research and Development Institute (NARDI). The TWG will provide technical coordination and support as some of the work under the TWG, such as NARDI, JUST and HKU, is expected to be executed.

## B. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

### The country has demonstrated strong commitment to putting in place measures for climate mitigation and adaptation, including NDCs, NAPCC, NAPC, NAMAN, TNC and the NAP. These measures are critical to its survival and the sustainable development of the country. The MoE has taken a leadership role in instituting many policy and institutional changes in response to climate change.

The MoE has been engaged with a range of readiness activities for GCF such as Strengthening its role as


---
**Page 49**
---

The NDA is based on the GCF Investment Framework, Improving Jordan’s Readiness to Access Finance for Climate Change and Green Growth in Jordan and has been involved with the preparation of the current project concept for the last several years. The prospect of financing from GCF has generated high level interest among the Ministry of Environment (MoE), the Jordanian Development Bank (JDB), local governments and NGOs consulted during the process of proposal preparation. The staff of MoE, the environment sector, and JDB have been trained in the GCF project cycle and are now prepared to participate in project activities and work closely with FAO and UNDPD to enhance their technical capacity and prepare a project concept for the opportunity to enhance the resilience of Jordan’s agriculture. The prospect of financing from GCF has brought forth interest not only from all external entities to work together, and provide financial and in-kind contribution. The opportunity to access GCF resources has put the limelight on the importance of climate change adaptation and its role in sustainable development.

### 97
While the resources requested from GCF are not significant, they are expected to be catalysts in demonstrating adaptive practices and technologies, incubating behavior change and bringing about a transformation in the manner in which Jordan plans, budgets for and implements climate adaptation. While the World Bank’s Climate Change Adaptation Program (CCAP) is currently being implemented (WorldBank, 2015), this classification does not consider the burden the country is facing of hosting refugees and there is an increasing need to address the environmental impacts of climate change in Jordan. The CCAP does not add to its debt burden and there are growing concerns about its public debt which increased to JD 29.51 billion at the end of July 2019 constituting 94.4 per cent of the estimated gross domestic product (GDP) for that year (Jordan Central Bank, 2019). The country is also facing a number of other challenges such as the regional conflicts in Syria and Iraq; the country’s main trading partners, seriously damaged Jordan’s trade balance and its economy. This has had an adverse effect on the country’s economic growth (WorldBank, 2020), including 1.3 million Syrian refugees[1] and refugees from a host of other countries as well as the influx of foreign workers. The influx of these groups has placed additional pressure on Jordan’s infrastructure and social services, but also its labour market and social cohesion. As a result, the country's macroeconomic indicators weakened, the fiscal deficit rose, and financing the external sector became challenging (WorldBank, 2019). In addition to this, the country is facing an economic slowdown from 2010-18. Jordan is likely to continue to face considerable economic pressure to address the immediate humanitarian needs of its population as well as implement plans for executing the Climate Change Adaptation (CCA). The investment by GCF is likely to be decisive in making the high value added investments of decision makers to deal with climate change risks.

### 98
The proposed BRCCJ project is very important for helping rural households deal with climate risks in the water scarce environment. It will contribute positively to national GDP, the agricultural sector as a critical importance in Jordan, for its socio-economic fabric and role in political stability, as well as its central role in addressing rural poverty and unemployment. The project will also help address the food security it creates[2]. Jordan currently imports more than half of its basic food crops, including almost all of its cereals. The request for financing to GCF is premised on the GCF’s understanding that it needs to prioritize a series of projects in order to achieve its goal of reducing poverty and vulnerability. Addressing climate change impacts on the agricultural sector could significantly interfere with progress of the country related to poverty reduction, due to the greater dependence of the rural poor on the sector, their lower ability to adapt to climate change, and the fact that they are more vulnerable to the adverse effects of climate change. The economic and rural growth economic are also expected to be adversely impacted. The COVID-19 pandemic is likely to worsen these pressures.


[1] Fawaz Al-Mohtadi’s Monthly Briefing, July 2019, Ministry of Jordanian.

[2] World Bank, Climate Change Adaptation Program (CCAP), The Economic Policy Council, Jordan.


---
**Page 50**
---

99. Replicating Jordan’s Combat (2018), climate adaptation and mitigation have proven challenging and remain highly conditional on the availability of financing and the lack of technical knowledge in the climate change domain. Current lack of finance is likely to impede implementation as Jordan’s general budget has prioritized other sectors, such as health and education. In addition, the current financial constraints on the government, climate action will require a shift in national planning and budgeting (alongside international funding) GCF investment in Jordan will help address these challenges by providing financing for projects that will benefit the agricultural producers in the Dead Sea Basin. Without GCF involvement to complement ongoing efforts and additional support from other donors, it is unlikely that the project would be implemented. The project will also help diversify water supply sources, enhance water productivity, and support vulnerable farmers and help the most disadvantaged families disproportionately impacted by climate change. GCF financing will support enabling activities for the project, including capacity building of local communities and institutions to ensure a sustainable basis across the country for climate adaptation. GCF finances will encourage wider participation and engagement in the project among all stakeholders, including women and youth. The proposed shift that the project will encourage has a long term impact on institutions and policy that will strengthen the climate adaptation capability in the country. There are no existing donors willing to invest in the selected Combate project. Jordan’s Ministry of Agriculture (MoA) is committed to providing 6.2 million USD from its own budgetary sources by contributing staff time, expertise, and resources. The MoA has also committed USD 2.06 million from their own budgets which USD 500,000 from UNDP and USD 1,000,000 from FAD will be co-financing the project. The GoJ is also contributing through tax exemptions on Goods and Services Tax (GST) for the Project.

B. Exit strategy and sustainability (max. 500 words; approximately / page)

100. The proposed project has been designed in close consultation with and involvement of relevant government agencies, technical line departments and communities in the project area. This has ensured that the components and activities proposed are in line with national policies and strategies with strong community ownership. The project will also ensure sustainability through a number of measures including building of local and community institutions, awareness and training of vulnerable households and private sector entities on climate change adaptation and mitigation, and capacity building for extension officers for delivering extension messages appropriate for adaptation, strengthening of policy and regulatory frameworks to support the implementation of the project, and ensuring that the results are sustained in the educational curricula and engagement of private sector in the supply of the full range of technologies, inputs and innovations. These measures are designed to ensure that the results of the interventions are sustainable over time through a combination of public and private sector involvement and by working side-by-side with existing links with private sector suppliers. The project will follow a demand-driven approach and expects that the benefits from the project will be sustained in the long term, as well as ensuring ownership of participating beneficiaries. The proposed strengthening of the coordination, knowledge generation, policy and regulatory mechanisms at the national level are expected to strengthen national capacity for Jordan’s MoA to implement sector specific strategies more efficiently, while delivering on resilience to climate change.

101. All investments in water infrastructure will be undertaken by local communities and local entities (e.g., municipalities). The roof-top water harvesting structures in public buildings will be maintained by the agency responsible for the operation and maintenance of the buildings. Individual households will maintain the roof-top water harvesting structures in their homes. Clear agreement and dedicated training on the responsibilities, roles and obligations of all parties involved are required to ensure that they make investments in public infrastructure. At the household level, identified systems will only require basic maintenance and monitoring by the beneficiaries themselves. The proposed interventions do not require any specific skills and can be executed with no risk for the health or security of the person. ### D.EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

#### D.1 Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

106. Climate change has adversely impacted food security and economic growth in vulnerable rural areas in Jordan. The poor in rural areas are expected to face the most severe consequences of climate change impacts on their livelihoods. In addition to the direct effects of climate change, such as increased temperatures, changes in rainfall patterns, and more frequent extreme weather events, there is also a strong expectation that the expected impacts of climate change, particularly the uncertain weather pattern and increased temperatures, threaten livelihoods and keep vulnerable people insecure. Poor families and households are the most vulnerable to these shocks because they have limited access to resources and coping mechanisms. In Jordan, 20% of its population experiences poverty transiently. Those at the margins of the poverty line are the most vulnerable to climate change impacts on their livelihoods. The poor in rural areas face greater vulnerability across all sectors. Water use in Jordan is evenly divided between agriculture (51%) and domestic sectors (49%). Climate change affects both sectors and can have potentially detrimental impact on women.

107. Research shows that the most significant issues Jordan faces due to climate change are related to water scarcity, food insecurity, and increased vulnerability of public infrastructure and food security. In rural areas of Jordan, women are more vulnerable to the effects of climate change than men.


---
**Page 56**
---

are, particularly because women are more dependent for their livelihoods on natural resources that are threatened by climate change. The project has the potential to make vulnerable households who suffer from water scarcity and livelihood vulnerability more resilient in facing the negative impacts of climate change. It will do this through a multi-pronged approach that includes increasing access to information, empowering women and leveraging their role as agents of change. The project has the potential to achieve two of the most significant outcomes for women: (i) increased food security; and (ii) increased resilience of the most vulnerable people, communities and regions; and (iii) increased resilience of health and well-being, and food and water security.

108. The project's specific outreach and fund-level impacts are expected to include the following:

* 212,416 people of which 47% will be direct and indirect women beneficiaries made aware of climate threats and related adaptation responses (PMF A.3.5)
* 83,743 people are expected to directly benefit from the project activities of which 33,684 or 40% are women beneficiaries
* 128,673 people are expected to indirectly benefit from the project activities of which 65,732 or 51% are women beneficiaries
* In the project area, the project is expected to benefit about 10% of the target population in the selected Government Districts (PMF A.4)
* 20,500 people including 9,220 women benefiting from the adoption of diversified, climate resilient livelihood options and practices (PMF A.3.6)
* 57,910 people including 28,212 women with year-round access to reliable and safe water supply despite climate shocks and stresses (PMF A.2.3)

109. The Fund Level Outcomes Expected from the project include the following:

* At least 40% of new policies and regulatory measures introduced for the water and agriculture sectors which provide an incentive for climate resilience from which about 167,818 people (82,902 women) nationally will benefit (PMF A.3)
* Increased use of climate information in water and agriculture sectors (PMF A-6.1)
* 30,000 household use climate smart mobile application or Information Communication Technology for Climate Change Adaptation (PMF A.7.4)
* 94,943 men and 39,974 women from vulnerable households, communities, businesses and public sector institutions will have access to information, materials, strategies and activities to respond to climate change and climate variability (PMF A-7.1).
* 135,623 men and 57,020 women made aware of climate threats and related appropriate responses (PMF A-4)
* At least 8 technologies introduced for climate adaptation of which 5 are useful for women (PMF -ACGT)

110. The project will contribute to several SDG targets which include the following:

* SGD 1: supporting farmers to adopt new technologies and practices that will increase their capacity to produce better and more productive crops and reduce poverty.
* SGD 2: helping to ensure food security, improve nutrition and reduce food insecurity and hunger;
* SGD 5; helping to empower women and enhance their capacity as agents of change thereby reducing gender inequality
* SGD 6: ensuring available and sustainable management of water by reducing withdrawals from surface and groundwater and ensuring good ambient water quality;
* SGD 11: making human settlements resilient and sustainable through climate adaptive planning and growth

## D.2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

### 111. This project will shift the current paradigm in water use and management for agriculture and domestic needs from the current unsustainable and vulnerable to a system that is adaptive and resilient to the identified climate change risks and impacts. Such shift will happen at different levels: (i) household; (ii) farm; (iii) landscape level; and (iv) national level.

### 112. At the household level, this paradigm shift involves changing the way in which people deal with increasingly unpredictable and scarce water resources (both for consumption and agricultural production). At household level, the project will support households to adopt a more sustainable approach to water management regarding the necessity of using water more wisely and the imminent danger for future generations. This is expected to bring about a sustainable behavior change in the way all household members think about water availability and use. The project will support this through training and awareness raising at the household level as a cornerstone of Jordan's strategy to reduce pressure on scarce freshwater resources, especially during the dry season. In addition, the project will support households with rooftop harvesting structures in new buildings; these are very recent and only implemented in Amman. The project will expand the use of rooftop rainwater harvesting structures in the selected Governorates to enhance the


---
**Page 58**
---

resilience of households to unpredictable and scarce freshwater resources. The project is promoting the use of these structures through their dissemination in public buildings, awareness raising campaigns and by incentivizing private households to invest through a grant based on household's vulnerability and economic potential. The project will also provide technical assistance for the implementation of water harvesting systems that will be fully financed by the households themselves through hiring of private contractors on their own and payment of a small fee. Enforcement of laws and regulations related to water use, including water tariff enforcement in the project Governors is expected to be a strong incentive to comply. The capacity of the previous sectoral level actors to provide technical assistance will be strengthened through technical assistance and they are expected to be increasingly engaged in promoting the technology.

133. The project will shift the paradigm in the agriculture sector from crop production per unit of land to crop productivity per unit of water. A sharper distinction will be made between water quality (which is a function of soil type, climate and other factors) and quantity (which can be increased or decreased using multiple times for suitable agriculture and agriculture given the added stress caused due to change in temperatures and precipitation levels in many areas of the country, especially the Dead Sea Basin. These new practices will be promoted through extension services, training of farmers and agricultural advisors on best use and use in the future. At the farm level, there is significant potential to promote a paradigm shift through the adoption of climate smart agriculture technologies. The project will support the development of tools to develop suitable extension materials to deliver appropriate messages to farmers and extension staff begin to think about thinking about how to adapt and potentially change their practices to take advantage of a range of climatic opportunities. Technologies include the development seeds of cereals and horticultural crops which are more drought resistant as well as a range of water saving technologies which have been tested and proven in the region. The project will also support the development of new irrigation systems that promote shift in the farming systems through adoption of these practices. Over time, the Government regulation and policy framework will encourage these practices by putting in place appropriate incentives and restrictions on the use of groundwater.

141. At the landscape level, there is potential to promote a paradigm shift in the way investments to enhance resilience to extreme climate events are identified and prioritized. While the need to adapt to extreme weather events has been recognized as an important element of the country's national strategy of strategic planning and investments which are part of the MWI's Climate Investment Plan are made in an ad hoc and random manner. To address this key capacity gap, the project will use the development of the landscape resilience investment plans as a vehicle to promote a paradigm shift at the level of the hydrological basin for capitalizing on the impact of climate change adaptation investments. The development of these landscape resilience investment plans will lead to a paradigm shift in the way MWI identifies and prioritizes investments. MWI will move away from ad hoc investment decisions towards a more strategic planning for areas such as the Dead Sea Basin and other key downstream areas.

151. At the national level, there is potential to promote a paradigm shift in policy and regulatory framework that anticipates adaptation, protect vulnerable households from climate risk and incentivizes transformative behavior change. While there is general awareness about the urgency of climate change adaptation in Jordan, there is need to address policy bottlenecks to promote the up-take of tools and practices for climate change adaptation at all levels. These include policies related to water pricing, protection of water tariffs, illegal water laws, green building codes, fodder subsidies and use of reclaimed water, etc. The Government has recently adopted a National Climate Change Strategy (NCCS) which is currently under review. A is very important and can be a powerful mechanism to initiate policy reform. There is also an opportunity for education and training institutions to provide greater understanding and advocacy in their curricula to help the next round of professionals and skilled workforce. The private sector represents one of the most important and under-utilized market-driven opportunities to scale up the use of climate adaptive


---
**Page 59**
---

### Social and institutional co-benefits

119. The project will be implemented through a collective and community-based approach which promotes the organization of potential participants in groups for awareness raising as well as for organizing users of reclaimed water into water user associations and organize farmers into groups for the execution of the project activities. This approach is expected to ensure that all stakeholders are involved in areas where the project will be implemented. The project will also be developing special programmes for school children, which will include a series of awareness raising sessions (see section 1.1.4). The awareness among children is expected to generate additional benefits as young people are more likely to become aware of climate change and will promote the climate change agenda as adults.

### Economic co-benefits

120. Additionally, investing in agriculture and water security of rural households will create new job opportunities for women and men through the implementation of agro-technological services as well as in the private extension services one (e.g. irrigation management, agro-technology). Furthermore, households will dispose of an average of 15 m3 of additional water that will also continue to maintaining personal hygiene standards and ensure a more effective and efficient control of COVID-19 at the household level.


---
**Page 60**
---

121. The project is expected to generate both quantitative and qualitative economic co-benefits. The benefits expected from each of the components are detailed in the sections below:

### Under Component 1: Climate Resilient Water Systems for enhanced water security:
- Output 1a: 7850 private households saving USD 127 per year (30 m3 saved per HH) after implementing water saving devices and rainwater harvesting systems. This results in a total savings of USD 984,600 per year.
- Output 1b: 200 on purchasing water tankers in the absence of harvested rainwater (between 22 m3 and 47m3 saved per HH). This results in a total saving of USD 50,000 per year.
- Output 1c: A comparison between USD 363 and USD 585 of expenditure on purchased water in the absence of harvested rainwater (between 87 m3 and 137m3 saved). This results in a total saving of USD 24,000 per year.
- Output 1d: 2,176 farmers generating around USD 620 of additional income per year from the sale of harvested rainwater. This results in a total savings of USD 1,358,000 per year.
- Output 1e: 400 households saving USD 90 per year (15 m3 saved per HH) after implementing water-saving devices and rainwater harvesting systems. This results in a total saving of USD 360,000 per year.

### Under Component 2: Climate change resilience for Environment, Health and Food Security:
- Output 2a: The use of the WASH and FIS Field days as an opportunity to lead to the adoption of technologies such as: a) 1,425 farmers benefiting with a 30% increase in return to family labor due to the implementation of water saving devices and rainwater harvesting systems. This results in a total savings of USD 687,000 per year.
- Output 2b: 900 women being reached to generate at least USD 130 additional value per year through the use of the new water-saving technologies. This results in a total saving of USD 117,000 per year.
- Output 2c: 50 farmers being trained to apply improved water management and climate adaptive techniques on 2520 hectares of trees with between 22% to 43% additional profit margins per hectare. This results in a total saving of USD 1,068,000 per year.
- Output 2d: 150 farmers being trained to apply improved water management and climate adaptive techniques on 973 hectares of land under rain-fed crops that reports between 15% to 72% additional margins per hectare. This results in a total saving of USD 4,680,000 per year.

### The increased availability of water from roof-top harvesting structures is expected to enable people to increase food production around the homesteads and improve nutrition, health and food security and generate additional income opportunities for women and youth. The importance of these changes are considered as one of the highest threats to health in Jordan. In 2005, a WHO/UNEP report estimated that 78% of Jordan’s population is at risk from water scarcity. This increased water availability from RWH is likely to generate improved health status of households, enhance hygiene – with subsequent reduction in the risk of disease transmission; reduce their health costs and reduce the pressure on public health services. The project will also provide opportunities for entrepreneurs trained in installing RWH and the increased employment opportunities for the installation and maintenance of RWH systems. This will be achieved through the sale of rainwater and reclaimed water: (i) economic impact of improved nutrition, health and food security; (ii) water subsidies public bill reduced; (iii) avoided downstream contamination from discharge of reclaimed water which has not undergone adequate treatment; (iv) increased employment opportunities for women and youth. The project will provide additional benefits such as economic benefits of improved nutrition, health and food security, increasing purchasing power and reducing the pressure on public health services. This will also generate additional employment generated through the backward and forward multiple effects in the project area.

### Gender-sensitive development impact
- The project is expected to reduce gender inequalities in climate change impacts. The project will expand women’s social and economic freedoms by focusing on their role as change agents, decision-makers and actors as well as increasing their access to resources required to meet their priorities. This will lead to the empowerment of women and youth through increased awareness about societal perceptions and lead to their greater participation in other spheres of public and professional life. A cadre of young Climate Wise Women will be developed as advocates, repositories of knowledge and


---
**Page 61**
---

technical support to support climate change adaptation in communities and informed interlocutors to influence the climate change agenda. Women will constitute 50 percent of the learners leading at the FFS. They will be involved as key decision-makers in the use of domestic water; they will be supported in their role as leaders in the management of water resources, including rainwater harvesting.

An enabling environment for the recognition and expansion of women’s role in climate change resilience will be created through the development of a gender action plan and the establishment of a gender focal point on consultations and knowledge dissemination events. Young men will be involved through creation of employment opportunities in the private sector, such as construction, plumbing and in private sector agencies selling water conserving technology and use of inputs that are more water efficient, climate adaptive and lead to sustainable farming practices.

D. Needs of recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

126. Vulnerability of Jordan and beneficiary groups: Jordan is one of the most water scarce countries in the world. The Kingdom has only two rivers that provide less than 3% of country’s climate vulnerability ranking (being the least vulnerable). Most of the country (92%) can be considered and to semi-arid, having an average precipitation of less than 200 millimeters. Consequently, the water sector is considered to be extremely important for Jordanian society and economy. The Kingdom has been facing a number of significant barriers for the sustainable development of the Kingdom.1 Rural poor areas in Jordan are the most vulnerable groups to climate change due to their dependence on rainfed agriculture and their sensitivity to temperature changes. The small holder farmers in the South are highly dependent on rainfall. As it is, the poverty rate among the four Governorates in the project area is above the national average (13.5%), with 24.2% in Ma'an, 21.7% in Karak and 20.8% in Tafila. The rural poor are also more vulnerable to climate change over time. According to the most recent official estimates, poverty rates have increased to an estimated 20% in 2016 from 15.4% in 2013. This is a significant increase and makes Jordan’s rural poor even more vulnerable to climate risks. There is a critical need to help build the resilience of rural communities in the South and make them less vulnerable to climate threats. Additional details are available in Annex, section 2.

127. Economic and social development needs of Jordan and affected population: Jordan economy and employment situation has been negatively impacted by the Syrian refugee crisis and COVID-19 is the latest setback. The regional conflicts in Syria and Iraq, the country’s main trading partners, have seriously disrupted trade flows and economic activity. The financial sector has also been severely weakened, the fiscal deficit rose, and financing the external sector became challenging. GDP growth, which had averaged 6.4 percent during 2009–15, fell below 2.5 percent over 2010-18 and is expected to stall in 2019 at around 1%. The unemployment rate has increased from an average of 7% between 2003 and 2015, based on the Household Expenditure and Income Survey of 2017–18. The rate has increased considerably since then to reach a record high of 14.6 percent in 2019. The proportion of households living below the poverty line was 14.4%. The influx of refugees, continues to exert tremendous pressure on public services and infrastructure, including education and health care systems and their labour market. The Government in increasingly concerned about the growing ranks of the unemployed. The unemployment rate is estimated to have reached 19.2% during the second quarter of 2019 (men: 17.1%; and women: 34.5%). This has been attributed to a combination of factors, including the Syrian refugee crisis. The country is taking some tough decisions to make the economy more business friendly and competitive, revive growth, and create jobs. The country needs assistance to build its resilience to climate change as it does not have the resources to undertake this investment on its own given the many challenges it faces.


---
**Page 62**
---

128. While National Adaptation Plan (NAP) clearly establishes objectives, key investment areas and potential pathways, the implementation is being jeopardized by the absence of public and private sources of financing. From the public perspective, the current context shows high fiscal imbalances and very high public debt levels. The government’s ability to mobilize domestic resources for climate change adaptation efforts as “efforts to broaden the tax base and mobilize revenues to support Jordan’s fiscal and development needs has only been partially successful.” Public sector capacity to implement adaptation measures is therefore limited—further international assistance will be critical in allowing for continued growth-enhancing reform.

The World Bank’s 2019 Climate Change Adaptation Strategy (CCAS) and the recently signed Enhanced Fund Agreement with Jordan on a Four-Year Extended Fund Facility but states that “financing support from Jordan’s international partners will be critical to support the government’s reform efforts,” and “the hosting of climate change adaptation measures in the country is fragile and justifies the requirement of the region with some measure of political stability. The situation is fragile and justifies the requirement of the region with some measure of concessional finance to help the country deal with its complex political, social and environmental issues.”

129. Needs for strengthening institutions and implementation capacity. There has been little examination of the existing policies and regulatory frameworks with regard to their impact on encouraging adaptation practices and measures. The current policy framework is not supportive of climate change adaptation efforts, purchasing polices of grains, animal feed and agriculture inputs and the policy of tariffs and fee-on-energy, water and transport. The government needs to review these policies and regulations and assess how they can become an incentive for increased adoption of adaptation technologies and practices. The academic and vocational training institutions in the country have not fully incorporated climate change adaptation into their curricula. There is a need to develop guidelines and regulatory assistance on how climate change adaptation can be integrated into the operational capacity of the country to implement adaptation measures. The local municipal level staff, civil servants and other stakeholders need to be trained on climate change adaptation practices and impacts of climate change scenarios and the coping strategies that need to be in place. The country needs to invest in these aspects of awareness raising and building capacity for increased resilience in the broad range of social, economic and environmental sectors.

D.5 Country ownership (page: 306 out of approximately 1 page)

130. Jordan has displayed strong ownership and commitment to changing the business as usual scenario and take serious note of the climate change threats posed to the country. The intended Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) and the National Adaptation Plan (2020) which was recently issued, and the Climate Change Action Plan (CCAP) are all in line with the national development strategy and the current project as key priorities including promoting gender equality and empowering women. The overall NDC is being developed by the Ministry of Environment (MoE), in collaboration with other sectoral academics who are also represented in the National Committee on Climate Change (NCCC). The existing GCF country programme was adopted by the NCCC in its meeting in February 2020. Jordan is currently preparing its second NDC, which will be submitted to the UNFCCC in 2021 and the revised NDCs. Jordan will undertake its Fourth National Communication (FNc) exercise in 2020 resulting in the submission of the fourth NDC in 2021. The MoE is now in the process of preparing a new policy on Climate Change as part of its FNC.

131. The MoE has also strengthened its internal capacity for climate change by establishing a climate change unit and so have the MoW and the MWW. These three key stakeholders identified focal points for the preparation of the NDCs, which are the MoE, MoW and MWW. The MoE will take overall ownership of the climate change agenda. The NCCC will act as the Project Steering Committee for the current project and facilitate its implementation. The NDA will play a coordinating role for presenting to the UNFCCC.

The NDA will be responsible for overall monitoring and supervision of the performance of the BRCCJ Project and the PMU will report directly to the MIE.

## The current project is well aligned with the GCF “climate program” which has identified adaptation as a priority area, covering four components: namely Water Resources and Water Security, Agriculture and Food Security; Ecosystems and Disaster Risk Reduction and Human Health. As a result of this alignment, several projects have been approved and are in the process of being undertaken such as Strengthening NDA of Jordan to deliver on GCF Investment Framework, Delivery Partner United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP) June 2018: USD 350,000; Project Title: Climate Change Adaptation for Sustainable Economic Growth in Jordan, Delivery Partner Global Green Growth Institute (GGGI) June 2018: USD 660,000. There are also two projects that have been approved and are awaiting funding from the GCF portfolio, namely the first and only country specific project which the MIE has included in the GCF Portfolio for submission during the first replenishment period of the GCF (2020-23). This clearly demonstrates the Govt’s commitment and overall support to the GCF.

### The Accredited Entity (AE) for the project is the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations, which is an international organization whose main goals are the eradication of hunger; food insecurity and malnutrition. It works in partnership with governments and civil society organizations on a wide range of natural resources. UNDP will undertake some key responsibility for the project as an EE. Both UNDP and FAO have considerable experience in Jordan in promoting climate resilient and sustainable agriculture, water management and disaster risk reduction. The AE has been selected by MIE to provide a catalyst role based on their comparative advantage in providing high quality technical assistance and implementing projects that are aligned with the GCF’s strategic objectives. UNDP will be responsible for the country with financing from EU; Government of Switzerland and others for climate change adaptation. UNDP is also in the process of implementing projects to assist with climate risks. FAO and UNDP have experience in Jordan in promoting climate resilient agriculture, water management and disaster risk reduction. The AE has been selected by MIE to provide a catalyst role based on their comparative advantage in providing high quality technical assistance and implementing projects that are aligned with the GCF’s strategic objectives. UNDP will be responsible for the country with financing from EU; Government of Switzerland and others for climate change adaptation.

### There is a large number of civil society organizations which are involved with a range of initiatives with respect to natural resource management and climate change adaptation. NGO's, grassroots movements and national organizations that represent local communities, farmers, women and other vulnerable groups have been actively involved in the development of Jordan’s National Adaptation Program of Action group consisting of seven agencies working on all aspects of Climate Change including WASH, DRR, Water Management, Agriculture, etc. The AE has provided technical assistance to these groups giving them voice to discuss challenges and gaps and will provide coordinated support to MIE in the development of the Climate Change Strategy paper. FAO was registered as an Accredited Entity (AE) with the GCF on 14th October 2016. Since then, FAO has been working closely with MIE to ensure that the project is fully under implementation. FAO’s capacity for implementation is further strengthened by its technical expertise in climate change and adaptation.

### The NAP process included private sector in the NCC and involved a range of entities that have leverage and represent the private sector such as the Jordan Chamber of Industry, Jordan Chamber of Commerce, Association of Banks, EDAMA Association, Jordan Green Building Council (JGBC) and others.

The private sector encompasses a wide and varied range of companies and businesses from large multinationals to small businesses. During stakeholders' consultations in Jordan's NAP process, the main barriers that emerged included lack of an enabling environment and market conditions as well as changing regulatory frameworks and policies. The private sector is expected to play a key role in increasing awareness of the benefits of engaging in climate change mitigation and adaptation as a viable business opportunity. This can be achieved by providing information on the financial and economic benefits, by supplying the range of services required for climate change mitigation and adaptation such as renewable energy technologies, water efficiency solutions, energy efficient buildings, climate adaptive technologies and inputs. The private sector will also be expected to provide insurance services, ICT/HA solutions, digital financial services as opportunities for business promotion and resilience building.

## D.5 Efficiency and effectiveness (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### Financial and Economic Analysis

#### 136. An analysis of the Value for Money Metrics of the Project shows that the project is highly justified based on both financial and economic analyses (Annex 3.6: Economic feasibility analysis). The project has a higher ERSR than most projects with a GCF cost per beneficiary of USD 2,054 compared to USD 18,790 for the average project in Jordan (Annex 19, Section 3.6). The project is designed to stimulate demand for climate adaptation practices and technologies and is expected to attract and crowd in private sector investments for climate adaptation.

#### 137. Financial profitability was assessed using twelve financial models that were developed based on the value chain analysis of the main crops (olives, grapes, dates, tomatoes) and livestock production competitiveness in Jordan and the current cropping pattern in the four governorates. These are given in Table 15. The models show that all crops except olives, grapes, date trees, and tomatoes have a range of improved technologies relevant for climate change expected yields and market prices compared to the baseline situation. Overall, all models show positive Net Present Values (NPV) and Financial Internal Rates of Return (FIRR). The project is estimated to generate NPVs in the range of JOD 10-25 million, up to JOD 14,666. Consequently, all models are considered profitable for a discount rate at 9%. According to Table 15, the Financial Internal Rate of Return (FIRR) varies between 7% and 38%, which summarizes profitability indicators for all the financial models. The project also demonstrated an Economic rate of return of 24.1% after applying conversion factors to obtain economic prices, incorporating economic mechanisms such as subsidies and taxes, and considering the discount factor at a rate of 9%. This is to be compared with total project costs (excluding investment costs already considered in the models in order to comply with GCF's cost per beneficiary USD 85.16 criteria), and the Benefit Cost Ratio is 2.34 with an economic discount rate estimated at 4% (given the current Jordanian 10-year bond yields and the CBU main interest rate), over the period of 30 years.

## Sensitivity Analysis

### Table 16: Sensitivity Analysis

Sensitivity analysis was conducted using different risk-occurrence scenarios. These included increases in project costs (10% and 20%), a reduction in project benefits (10% and 20%), and combined scenarios of both cost and benefit reductions. The analysis also considered the impact on project benefits for the first two years from project benefits (1 and 2 years) and the reduction in benefits by 50% every 2 and 3 years due to the continued increase in costs.

The results show that the project is sensitive to reduction in benefits.

Assumptions
- Baseline data is the most recent available.
- The baseline survey was conducted in 2014/2015.
- It is assumed that 4000 of the households surveyed are rural poor and live within a radius of two km from the field site.
- It is assumed that 30% of these households include women (i.e., those who have children under five years old, or those who are heads of household).
- Baseline data includes information on access to water supply services in the last three months and climate change adaptation options (including agricultural practices, nutrition, etc.).
- It is assumed that 50% of the rural poor live within a radius of two km from the field site.
- It is assumed that 30% of these households include women (i.e., those who have children under five years old or are heads of household).
- The baseline survey was conducted in 2014/2015 and the mid-term survey in 2017, with final data to be collected by 2023.
- Absence of clean water is a major barrier for rural poor women. GIZ (2017) found that 86% of rural poor households lack access to safe drinking water supply services.

## Health programs

The project will be based at the Ministry of Health and Population (MoHP) and will work with other relevant government agencies, including the National Environment Agency, to ensure that climate change adaptation and mitigation measures are integrated into existing health programs.

The impact will be a reduction in the vulnerability of communities to climate-related disasters and an increase in their adaptive capacity. The project will also contribute to improved health services or health infrastructure through the development of new policies, guidelines, or standards for disaster risk management.

## Activity 1.1.2.3 Technical assistance to promote the use of reclaimed water, including building trust with farmers and Water User Associations

### Develop Tools & Awareness

This activity promotes safe reuse of reclaimed water among targeted beneficiaries (farmers, WUA members, and local government) through training, awareness raising, and safe re-use of reclaimed water infrastructure. It includes both men and women in the process.

- **Develop Tools:** Create a service provider
- **Awareness:** Carry out non-personal communication and outreach to promote the use of reclaimed water among farmers especially those who are involved in agriculture.

## Activity 1.1.3 Establish plan objectives and criteria

A service provider is contracted to work closely with an international water specialist to develop a landscape resilience investment plan, including selecting and prioritizing landscape resilience actions. The definition of decision criteria will include the involvement of stakeholders through a stakeholder mapping exercise. At the broadest level, the plans aim are to:

- Increase landscape resilience
- Increase groundwater recharge and water availability

## Local engagement and dissemination process

The project will work with all the expressions of the civil society in the provinces as well as national and local media to develop a common set of approaches and actions to engage with communities and areas at different levels. This will include reaching out through community leaders, people and mainstream key citizens to share best practices and technologies as well as providing them with advice and/or find additional learning materials.

### Technical Assistance

To enhance local administration's and private sector actors' capacities to comply with the new construction and water saving policy frameworks

The project will support local administrative bodies (provinces and municipalities) as well as private sector actors such as masons, plumbers, electricians, etc. by promoting the inclusion of gender-sensitive practices in their daily work tools and practices that will contribute to reducing water consumption and climate change adaptation actions within communities and households. This will include providing technical and technical assistance on the new policy frameworks and policies for compliance with the limitation and bottlenecks procedures as well as promoting climate change adaptation actions, energy efficiency and saving actions through the green building projects.

### Technical assistance and training

To local institutions and civil society organizations

The project will transfer to national and local NGOs and CBOs the best practices introduced by the project in cooperation with other project to enhance their capacity for development processes to show leadership and groups to expand the benefits of knowledge shared and trained on technologies

* Coordination and gender-inclusive consultation with all the expressions of the civil society
* Organization of logistics at national level
* Execution of the local training modules
* Monitoring and evaluation plan

At least 50 local communities will be reached and executed.

E.7. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

139. A Monitoring and Evaluation System will be established for the BRCJC project in keeping with the key performance indicators of GCF on the Performance Measurement Frameworks (PMF) designed to measure the key outputs and outcomes envisaged at the project level. The PMF is described in detail in Annex 8, below along with their timelines and reporting requirements. More details are provided in Section 8, Annex Z and Annex AA. The PMF assumes over-arching data for all indicators from the monitoring and reporting, and providing key oversight of data and tools required for the strategic planning and resource decision-making. An M&E Unit will be established within the PMM and Monitoring and Evaluation Specialists will be recruited to support this function. The M&E Unit will also be responsible for developing an M system which will geo-reference all activities using FAO’s Remote Sensing application Earth Watch, as well as other tools such as GPS and GIS. A MIS will be developed in parallel with the PMM system will also be used for tracking beneficiaries over time and assessing impact.

140. The PMM will formulate an Annual Work Plan & Budget based on the annual physical targets indicated in the proposal and monitor performance against the plan. Formats will be developed for each of the reports namely the monthly statistical report, the quarterly statistical and narrative report and the Annual Performance Report (APR). The APR will be prepared by the project team with inputs from GCF/FAO will take the lead in the preparation and submission of the APRs. In order to report on the progress made towards achieving the outputs and outcomes envisaged at the project level, the PMM will prepare using sex-disaggregated data. APRs will also contain a narrative with updates on the progress of each output and outcome envisaged at the project level. APRs will include an update on the status of the achievement of the GCF Gender Action Plan (GAP) targets. The APRs, together with the Annual Work Plan (AWP), will form the basis for each annual year planning exercise and the preparation of the AWP. These will be presented to GCF in order to support strategic role and to secure transparency and evidence-based strategy development.

141. All partners such as private contractors, service providers, technical experts and UNDP as an EE for some of the activities will be responsible for maintaining records on the standard format designed as part of the project MIS and the reporting system. Partners will be trained in the use of the geo-referencing tool and will be required to submit monthly reports on their progress against the agreed targets. They will also adapt the reporting formats developed by the project. All implementing partners will be required to provide sex-disaggregated data for all indicators as part of their reporting responsibilities. The PMF will include gender-sensitive indicators drawn from the Climate Smart Agriculture Source Book (FAO, 2009) identified in the proposal and will also include other indicators that are relevant to the project. Partners will be required to annually report on physical and financial progress but also on key performance indicators and outputs if needed. The contacts with each partner will specify their reporting responsibilities, the frequency of the reports and the format for these reports. All partners will be required to provide feedback on how they view the Gender Action Plan which is an integral part of the proposal and report on the implementation and results achieved against the targets set out in this plan. All implementing partners will also provide requisite sex-disaggregated data for the mid-term and final impact evaluations and make themselves available and open for any external financial and performance audits.


---
**Page 82**
---

# Gender Indicators to Monitor FFS

The number of gender-responsive technologies applied for climate-smart agriculture.

The rate of participation of men and women in the selection process for a range of technologies.

The number of women and men participating in climate-smart agriculture-related Farmer Field Schools or farmer-to-farmer extension activities.

Perceptions of women and men on the sufficiency of the climate smart agricultural technologies provided to them.

Percentage change in crop yield per hectare and year as a result of the climate smart agricultural technologies provided to them.

The number of women and men who have access to weather and climate information services and price information services.

The changes in the labor burden of women and men (e.g., number of persons requiring a significant reduction in the time spent for collecting water).

The number of women in leadership and decision-making roles or positions in their communities.

143. The M&E unit will be responsible for periodic checks on some of the data collected from different project activities, including those related to gender equality. The M&E unit will also collect feedback from the relevant Ministers to conduct sex-disaggregated beneficiary feedback sessions in the field. The MoI will be involved in the feedback from those participating in the RWH investments, the use of reclaimed water and other climate-smart agricultural technologies. The Climate Watch Unit (CWU) at the Ministry of NARC will be responsible for the feedback sessions with participants of the FFS and field days. MoI will be responsible for the feedback session on the use of reclaimed water and other climate-smart agricultural technologies.

As a first step, the number of women and men who downloaded the app will be tracked along with those who are viewing the content in the audio, video and text format. The trends in usage and adoption of the app will also be monitored through the data collected from the app.

The women extension staff from the project Governmental unit will assist in conducting sessions with the Climate Watch Unit at the Ministry of NARC to collect feedback on the use of reclaimed water and other climate-smart agricultural technologies by women and men. The CWU will provide technical assistance for performance as agents of change. Those providing technical assistance will be required to provide detail reports which track the core indicators, the impact and outcome and output level indicators specified in the plan.

The M&E database will be maintained by the PMI and information will be supplied by all service providers on a monthly basis or in real-time if a link can be established with the central control site at the M&E unit. Within the first quarter of the second year, when activities have been initiated and sufficient outreach has been achieved, the project will be able to collect data from all service providers implementing the project and will be monitored through consolidated remote sensing practices or geopatial analysis. This is expected to yield a better understanding of trends and patterns and make the analyses more meaningful in understanding the impact of the project on women and men as well as the gender equality issues in project activities. Data collected through reports prepared by service providers implementing partners and families with beneficiaries will be sex-disaggregated.


---
**Page 83**
---

144. Each funding entity will also establish a mechanism for sex-disaggregated beneficiary feedback and demonstrate how they have incorporated the feedback in improving their implementation approach. A beneficiary feedback will also be preprogrammed by each implementing partner on an annual basis and will form part of the Annual Performance Report (APR). The APRs will be made available to the public, and will be communicated by each funding entity to the participants so that the participant can directly access the information. The APRs will be published in accordance with the requirements set out in the APIS. Funding entities will follow the procedures for Complaints Related to the Organization’s Environmental and Social Standards where a specific complaint is made. These will be sent to the PMU, which will form part of the Secretariat’s complaints mechanism (see Section 143). The APRs will also include information on gender equality and social inclusion as per the FAO ESIS (2016).

145. The project will commission a Baseline Survey by a third party in year one against which subsequent changes and impact will be measured. The project will also undertake surveys at mid-term and at completion to assess the performance of the project, draw important lessons and incorporate beneficiary feedback. The first survey will be conducted within six months after the baseline survey. A final evaluation will be carried out by an independent third party in year four. A final impact assessment will be undertaken to assess the overall impact of the project on the beneficiaries. This will include a review of the project’s performance against its objectives and outputs, including the adaptation practices and assess the overall impact on the paradigm shifts outlined in the project log-frame and the indicators of resilience outlined at the impact level. All surveys and impact assessments will be sex-disaggregated. The lessons learned from the baseline survey, mid-term survey, and the final evaluation under Action Plan will be captured in the initial and subsequent surveys and findings. The Surveys will be conducted by an independent third party with the assistance of the PMU. The final evaluation will be conducted by external parties under OED supervision to ensure independence and quality.

146. In accordance with the AMA between FAO and GCF, the OAO Office of Evaluation will be responsible for the independent interim and final evaluations. The evaluations will be conducted using a question-driven approach that will focus on the project’s performance against its objectives and outputs, among others. The interim evaluation will be instrumental in contributing – through operational and strategic recommendations – to the achievement of the project’s objectives during the first two years of the remaining period of the project. The final evaluation will assess the relevance of the intervention, its overall performance, as well as sustainability and scalability of results, differential impacts and lessons learned. The evaluation will also assess the extent to which the project has contributed towards achieving the ultimate goal of achieving a paradigm shift in adaptation to climate change in Jordan. Special attention will also be given to gender equality and social inclusion. The evaluation will draw on mixed-methods, including innovative partnerships leading to impact and on the establishment of a clear pathway to transformational change. To measure attributable changes, the evaluation will draw on mixed-methods, using qualitative methods (e.g., case studies) in combination with quantitative methods (e.g., surveys), as well as in combination with counterfactual analysis (e.g., quasi-experiment methods, depending on the existence of a control group). The evaluation will also be based on secondary data collected by the project team and information prepared by project staff. The final evaluation will draw on results of the impact evaluation and both processes impact and final evaluation will be carried out under OED’s responsibility. Careful attention will be paid to gender equality and social inclusion in all stages of the evaluation process, with respect to gender will be especially highlighted in the assessments and evaluations with key lessons drawn for the future.

147. Learning and knowledge management: The Project will also synthesize the lessons that emerge from the Programme/sector section in the APIS, including lessons on mainstreaming gender. These lessons will be shared with the NDA and the NCCC to enable them to incorporate them in the strategies and plans being developed by the country in its NAP and other key strategy documents. The project will work with
