# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## Selected Risk Factor 1

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Medium | Low |

### Description

149. Expected contribution from the Government could be jeopardized due to the budget deficit. The Government faces a tough balancing act as it tries to reduce its debt burden by lowering its borrowing, cut spending and raise revenue. In 2018, the IMF recommended that Jordan should consider measures to impose new taxes in its proposed 2020 budget and intends to seek other means, such as a crackdown on tax evasion, to increase revenues. The Government has been reluctant to implement these reforms due to the Government's reluctance to impose new taxes since its past experience when demonstrations were triggered in 2018 when steep tax hikes pushed by the IMF came into effect. At the same time the Parliament has repeatedly rejected any attempt at raising taxes and instead has called for a cut in spending, and urged the government to reduce sales taxes on essential food items to help low-income Jordanians cope with rising prices. The Government's reluctance to raise revenues is likely to continue as it remains reliant on continuing international donor inflow to support Syrian refugees and develop physical infrastructure in areas of displacement. Jordan’s government is thus exposed both to internal and external pressures.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

150. Due to the limited fiscal space that the Government has and its reluctance to borrow additional finance given its high debt ratio, the project does not impose any additional financial burden on the Government. 66% of the Government's expenditure is committed for this project and is kindred and entails using its existing resources. The Project will be funded from Farm Sector Savings and other innovative technologies to avoid overhauling the extension Staff. Government’s direct contribution (estimated at USD 2 million) will be used as a match funding for the project. This amount represents only 10% of the total part of the WMY Investment Plan for 2015-2025 and there is no any other direct financial contribution from the GCO which could have placed an additional fiscal burden on it. On the contrary, water saving generated by the Project will be used to increase the profitability of the existing irrigation systems. The profitability was verified with different scenarios, considering the shortfall of funds of the Government directly committed for this project and only 25% of the total number of hectares (Water Harvesting Systems). Thus, the level of risk with respect to the project dependence on Government funds has been eliminated.

# Selected Risk Factor 3

## Description

151. Over the last two decades, the Jordanian government has been committed to women's rights and increasing female participation in the workforce. However, only 15% of women enter the workforce despite relative gender equality in educational achievement in Jordan. Apart from other factors, it appears that women are more likely than men to be employed in informal sectors or as domestic workers, which could limit their opportunities for employment in the country and could also limit women's participation in project activities. Social, institutional and material barriers get in the way of women feeling safe at work and achieving justice against violations committed at the workplace. Women may also face gender-based violence (GBV) such as sexual harassment or even facing sexual harassment. The project expects to involve women as direct beneficiaries of its activities. It will ensure that women are included in all aspects of the project, including selection of participants, use of reclaimed water, as participants in FFS, field days and as agents of change who can influence how women access resources and services. Women Extension Agents from the extension service and technical specialist from NARC are also expected to participate as Facilitators and Field Day Specialists in training the participants of FFS and other activities. The project will ensure that at least 50% of the facilitators and field day specialists are women. Thus, it is very important that women feel safe in participating in project activities.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

152. The project has a strong focus on inclusion of women or socially marginalized groups. The project will ensure that women facilitators and women participants feel secure in their participation in project activities. This will be achieved by ensuring that women are included in all aspects of the project, including selection of participants, use of reclaimed water, as participants in FFS, field days and as agents of change who can influence how women access resources and services. Women Extension Agents from the extension service and technical specialist from NARC are also expected to participate as Facilitators and Field Day Specialists in training the participants of FFS and other activities. The project will ensure that at least 50% of the facilitators and field day specialists are women. Thus, it is very important that women feel safe in participating in project activities.

The project will also organize sessions with women on an annual basis to invite their feedback on their participation, security and safety and other aspects of participation. In addition, the project will design a warning system for women who face gender-based violence (GBV) at work or elsewhere. The women can complain to lodge. The project will also make arrangements for a grievance redress mechanism at the level of the Governorate and the FAO Sub-Office. The complaints received through the FAS system that are not resolved at the local level or in case someone does not feel comfortable reporting at the Governorate level, they can be referred to the FAO Sub-Office. The records of cases will be used for the purpose. The project will ensure that these arrangements in all locations where project activities are conducted through a flier and the hotline numbers.

G. GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

### G1. Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

In accordance with FAO and GCF's Environmental and Social Policy (ESSP), the BRCCJ underwent an environmental and social assessment following FAO’s environmental and social safeguards (E&SSA, 2010). The project will not have a significant adverse impact on the environment or society. On the contrary, the project will build the adaptive capacity of communities and institutions in Jordan, address the water scarcity problem through improved water management practices, and enhance water management systems as well as agricultural resources to climate change. Project components were identified through a consultative process, and are prioritized in Jordan’s Climate Change policy and its National Adaptation Program (NAP). The project will contribute to the achievement of Jordan’s NAP goals on conventional water sources, building adaptive capacity of farming households and a range of institutions at the national level. The project is consistent with Jordan’s Water Sector Strategy and contributes to an important element of climate change adaptation in the NAP and is mainstreamed in project interventions with specific activities also identified to promote women's agency.

The ESSM identifies policy triggers for the project, screening criteria for activities, environmental and social safeguards, and mitigation measures. The ESSM outlines potential risks. Mitigation actions will avoid, minimize and mitigate negative impacts during project implementation and operation. Mitigation actions will be taken in accordance with Jordan’s Water Sector Strategy and NAP goals on conventional water sources.

The ESSM also sets out the modalities for stakeholder engagement, and the procedure and process for dealing with complaints, through the Grievance Redress Mechanism.

### G2. ESFM

The ESFM will be disclosed on relevant portals, and shared with stakeholders during stakeholder engagement activities to ensure transparency of project activities. Consultations with stakeholders during project execution will take place yearly, at the time of the preparation of the Annual Work Plan (AWP). The ESFM will also be made available for public consultation in accordance with all stakeholders, including at the national, Governmental and community levels. During these stakeholder consultations, the Grievance Redress Mechanism will also be presented and explained.

In order to ensure a smooth and effective ESFM process, there will be a technical specialist responsible for ESFM (who will be part of the project team and stakeholder engagement) who will interact on a regular basis with key stakeholders and be available to respond to any grievances.

Proposed project investments are designed to have positive social and environmental benefits; the proposed project is not considered as Category III (largely due to works associated with water resources. FAO ESSG suggested otherwise.

### G3. Natural resources management

Risks related to the installation of rooftop rainwater harvesting systems, including the construction of tanks for storage and distribution of hydraulic structures built to maximize use of reclaimed water from the Wastewater Treatment Plants, Jordanian legal frameworks, and international standards (e.g., WHO Guidelines for the safe use of wastewater, excreta and greywater) will be followed, as applicable. Best practice for optimal rainwater harvesting, including process and materials will be implemented; all left-over construction material will be disposed of at an appropriate site in an appropriate manner.

### G4. Plant genetic resources

Drought-tolerant and water efficient seed varieties will be used, such as cultivars of barley and fruits. These varieties are old varieties and have been tested and patented by NCARE under the MoA; if and as applicable, internal FAO clearance will be sought

## G

### 162. ESS (decent work) Potential risks could be related to equitable benefits from project activities.

To address this, project activities specifically target women and also youth (ref: Gender Action Plan). Occupational health and safety standards will apply for all workers regardless of gender or age. Where the project hires workers, employers' rights as per UNFAD standards will be respected. The employment of project workers will be based on the principle of equal opportunity and fair treatment, and there will be no discrimination in terms of recruitment and employment relationship.

### 163. Annex E provides the Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP) and Annex F provides a summary of consultations and the stakeholder engagement plan.

#### G.2 Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

In keeping with GF's Gender Policy, the project design was based on the understanding that gender relations, roles and responsibilities exercise important influence on women's and men's access to and control over resources, which in turn affects their ability to adapt to climate change risks.

### 165. Women are among the most vulnerable to climate change as they have the main responsibility for many of the tasks for which there is a much greater reliance on natural resources. This includes their role in collection and use of domestic water, livestock feed and food for the households. Women are disproportionately affected by climate change because of their limited access to productive assets, decision-making processes and limited mobility.

In the country, almost 61% of female-headed households are food insecure or malnourished compared with only 39% of male-headed households. This has significant social, economic, and political barriers that negatively affect coping capacities. However, they have a critical role to play as educators, caretakers, practitioners, and agents of change in climate resilience at the household level.

### 166. The Gender Action Plan of the project aims to have a transformative impact on women's role in climate change through their engagement in the agriculture and water sectors in the country. It also ensures that 47% of all direct and indirect beneficiaries are women (in CCA). The ESMAP identifies three areas where awareness raising is needed at multiple levels: (i) women's practical experience as a form of access to water is addressed; (ii) vulnerable women are prioritized and provided access to water; (iii) women's 'voice' is included in socio-economic feasibility for developing water sources and in assessing the impact of climate change.

# G.3 Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

## 167. FAO will be the Accredited Entity which will execute the project in accordance with FAO rules, regulations, policies, and procedures. The financial management of this project will be guided by management and rules and regulations as relevant provisions in the Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA) signed between GCF and GCF. These rules and regulations were reviewed by GCF and deemed suitable for use within the context of the project.

## 168. FAO has deployed an Oracle based resource planning system termed the Global Resources Management System (GRMS) which provides FAO personnel with travel, human resources, procurement and finance functionalities. The system has improved the flow of financial information and supports financial monitoring and reporting. The GRMS is a single instance system that allows for the separation of expenditure by donor and project. The GRMS also maintains a Chart of Accounts that allows for the separation of income and expenditure by donor and project. The GRMS provides a comprehensive view of all transactions, including those related to travel, personnel, procurement and summarized to facilitate internal management and meet external reporting and audit requirements.

## 169. Directly procured by FAO will be undertaken in the project in accordance with the FAO manual procedures for procurements of Goods, Works and Services (S02) and for sub-contracting the delivery of specific services or goods to third parties. The procurement plan is based on the approved project results as provided for in the operational partners implementation methodology (T01). The project will be subject for FAO’s internal procurement oversight unit (POU) which monitors compliance with the procurement plan has been provided based on the project budget (Annex 4) and the expected timelines (Annex 5) using the FAO procurement procedures and thresholds and is given in Annex 10. This plan has been coordinated with UNDP’s procurement plan for the same period. In accordance with the UNDP manual of procurement, a plan by UNDP is also attached at Annex 10A if required. Responsibility for UNDP’s procurement management is shared between the Office of Procurement and Supply (OPS) and project management – is done locally. The head of the Country Offices, the Resident Representative (or his or her designee) oversees the process. The Country Office will enter into contracts with vendors, which may be carried out by FAO personnel or UNDP staff. The POU provides oversight to ensure that procurement undertaken by UNDP units complies with relevant guidelines and that procurement risks are properly anticipated and mitigated. With offices all over the world and with a broad mandate to work for sustainable rural development, FAO buys both goods and services to be able to carry out projects and programmes together with partners.

# Secretariat’s assessment of FP155

## Overall assessment of the Secretariat

The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| The project has a strong gender focus through which climate-wise women will be trained in climate-resilient agriculture practices and will be able to introduce these practices into the agriculture sector in Jordan and can be a key change agent in transforming behaviour towards adopting adaptive practices at household level. | The reclaimed water treated under the previous project is not suitable for fodder crops. This means only those farmers who grow alfalfa and other fodder could benefit from this project. If the project relies on imported water, when drought affects the eastern Mediterranean region it will result in a sharp drop in livestock numbers including sheep, goats and cattle. Waterwaste harvesting systems (RHS) are not suitable for domestic use and for irrigating food crops according to Jordanian water quality standards. |
| The water conservation component will help after household behaviour to encourage them to reduce their water consumption by adoption of water saving devices and rainwater harvesting systems (RHS). | The theory of change links three disparate elements: the introduction of resilient cropping systems, policy reform to incentivize water use efficiency, and the effective management of groundwater. |

The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions as specified in annexure sheet and addendum X103 titled “List of proposed conditions and recommendations”.

## Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

### Project background
This is Jordan's first single-country adaptation project submitted to GCF for possible funding, and is designed to further the objectives of the country's Climate Change Policy and


---
**Page 99**
---

National Adaptation Plan: Jordan has a severe water scarcity problem, which is further exacerbated by unsustainable agricultural practices and inefficient water use. Many vulnerable communities in the project sites are poor and dependent on agriculture for livelihoods. The project targets these communities to improve their access to safe drinking water through an associated entity (AE), the adaptation focus of the project was further refined and clarity on beneficiaries selection criteria was provided. The AE is a joint venture between the Ministry of Environment (MoE), Ministry of Agriculture (MoA), and Ministry of Water and Irrigation (MWI) of Jordan and the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP). It builds on the established institutional arrangements for water management in Jordan, including national committees such as the National Climate Change Committee (NCCC) and the Working Group on Climate Adaptation.

4. Jordan is one of the most water scarce countries in the world and climate change is further exacerbating and acuity due to increasing temperatures and reduced/more erratic rainfall patterns. The project will address these challenges by improving access to safe drinking water from wastewater recycling and rainwater harvesting, and by reducing demand on groundwater resources through improved irrigation practices, efficient use of water-saving technologies, and by enhancing vulnerability, and through household adoption of water efficiency devices. Innovative training programmes for the beneficiaries will be introduced to promote sustainable cropping and household water-saving devices.

5. Total project costs are estimated to be USD 102.32 million. This project requests for a grant from GCF in the amount of USD 67.89 million (66% of total cost). The remainder of the plan is committed to providing USD 6.2 million (19 per cent), and FAO and UNDP will co-finance USD 2.6 million (1 million and 1.6 million, respectively, representing 4 per cent of total costs). The beneficiaries are expected to provide USD 0.64 million for investments at the household level on an in-kind basis.

### Component-by-component analysis

#### Component 1: Climate resilient water systems for enhanced water security (total cost: USD 19.65 million; USD 147.8 million or 75 per cent to be funded by GCF)

The project will implement a range of climate-resilient technologies, such as rooftop rainwater harvesting, use of water-saving domestic devices and use of reclaimed water that will be upcycled in the project area, which would be used as an entry point for promoting awareness and orientation. The AE will provide technical assistance to all households that will support the installation of BHS (10–20 m²) covering 7,850 household rooftops and 400 public buildings. Mitigation measures will be put in place to ensure that environmental and social risks are adequately managed in the final design and construction phases. The AE will also provide training for households on how to use BHS and ensure that environmental and social risks are adequately managed in the final design and construction phases. The AE will also provide training for households on how to use BHS and ensure that environmental and social risks are adequately managed in the final design and construction phases.

A service provider who will be competitively selected among non-governmental organizations (NGOs) will be responsible for providing technical assistance, including water-efficient water use in homes and in the public sphere such as schools, mosques and other public places. The AE will provide training to households on how to use BHS and ensure that environmental and social risks are adequately managed in the final design and construction phases. In coordination with the Project Management Unit (PMU), the awarded contractors will provide and install RHS in selected households. The beneficiaries that hold rights from BHS will be expected to contribute 10 per cent of their total water savings towards the project cost. This includes single-headed households, refugee and poor households, and those reliant solely on agriculture.

### III. Assessment of the performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: High

12. The project is expected to reach 212,416 people of which 47 per cent will be direct and indirect water beneficiaries made aware of climate threats and related appropriate responses. 85,974 people are likely to directly benefit from the project activities of which 33,004 or 40 per cent are women beneficiaries.


---
**Page 101**
---

3.2 Paradigm shift potential

* Scale: High

13. The project will shift the paradigm in the agriculture sector from looking at crop productivity as a function of water availability per unit of area. A sharper focus can be made between water quality (saline, brackish, underground saline, brine, reclaimed, etc.) and how each kind can be more effectively used multiple times for suitable agriculture and wastewater reuse. This will lead to increased agricultural productivity at lower water use levels in many areas of the country, especially the Dead Sea basin.
14. At the farm level, there is significant potential to promote a paradigm shift through the adoption of appropriate technologies. The project will contract MoA and provide technical assistance to develop suitable extension materials to deliver appropriate messages to farmers and extension officers on how best to use water in an increasingly unpredictable climate.
15. At the household level, this paradigm shift involves changing the way in which people deal with increasingly unpredictable and scarce water resources (both for consumption and agriculture). The project will provide training and awareness raising sessions regarding the necessity of using water more wisely and the imminent need to adapt to a new reality.
16. At the national level, there is potential for a paradigm shift in climate policy and the regulatory framework to incentivize adaptation, protect vulnerable households from climate risk and ensure sustainable development.
17. As water users become accustomed to the use of recycled wastewater, there is potential for regional expansion of wastewater recycling facilities as an asset class for private investment in arid countries.

3.3 Sustainable development potential

* Scale: Medium-high

The project is expected to help realize broader benefits with respect to several of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) in Jordan, helping vulnerable groups adapt to climate change is expected to have an impact on their ability to combat poverty and hunger, enhance access to safe drinking water and sanitation, promote gender equality, reduce inequalities and promote climate action.

3.4 Needs of the recipient

* Scale: High

Jordan is one of the most water scarce countries in the world and has an ND-GAIN index ranking of 85 out of 101 countries for climate vulnerability (ranking 1 being the least vulnerable). The country’s average annual precipitation is only about 275 millimeters, with average precipitation of less than 200 millimeters. Consequently, the water sector is considered to be extremely vulnerable to climate change and the scarcity and variability of the resource to be one of the significant barriers for the sustainable development of Jordan.

3.5 Country ownership

* Scale: High

The current project well aligns with the GCF country programme, which has identified four priority projects centred around four components; namely water resources and water security; agriculture and food security; ecosystems; disaster risk reduction and climate change adaptation. The project is also aligned to the priorities of the MoU.

* The ND-GAIN Country Index summarizes a country’s vulnerability to climate change and other global challenges in combination with its readiness to improve resilience.


---
**Page 102**
---

3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

Scale: High

The project’s cost-effectiveness indicators, compared with other GCF funded rural development projects in the region, show that it has a relatively higher economic rate of return than most projects with a GCF cost per beneficiary of USD 117.70 and total cost per beneficiary of USD 235.40. The project also demonstrated an economic rate of return of 24.1 per cent after applying conversion factors to obtain economic prices, incorporating economic models to include benefits of water saving interventions. The project’s net present value is positive when measured with total project costs (excluding investment costs already considered in the model).

### IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

The project seeks to increase Jordan's resilience to climate change through improving water use efficiency in the agriculture sector. It proposes to build the capacity of communities and institutions, address the needs of vulnerable groups, and increase the resilience of water management systems. The project also aims to improve the quality of life for rural populations by addressing an existing lack of infrastructure, limited capacity of rural farming households, and inadequate access to safe drinking water and sanitation services; (ii) climate change resilience for enhanced livelihoods and food security; and (iii) scaling up climate adaptation.

The GCF designated implementing partners (UNDP and the Ministries of Environment, Agriculture and Water and Irrigation of Jordan) anticipate that the project will deliver positive social and environmental impacts. The project is expected to have a moderate level of potential negative impacts. Its classification as category B (moderate risk) is due to potential risks associated with construction of water harvesting structures and expanding size of irrigation schemes. The Secretariat has not categorised it, and it is within the AE’s environmental and social accreditation level.

environmental and social management framework (ESMF) that identifies risks and policy triggers for the project, screening criteria and analysis of environmental and social (EAS) risks and impacts and mitigation measures to address the identified risks and impacts. 28. ES3 - resource efficiency and pollution prevention: The project will promote efficient use of resources and prevent pollution during the construction and operation of the facilities. This includes the use of locally available materials for construction, installation of harvesting structures in houses and in public buildings. During construction, measures to minimize dust generation and control noise levels will be taken through excavation activities at the project site; avoiding construction during periods of anticipated rainfall (if applicable) or other weather conditions that may cause dust generation is also possible.

28. ES4 - community health, safety and security: The activities of the project are not expected to have adverse impacts on the health and safety of the communities. The rainwater harvesting structures will be constructed in such a way as to minimize risks associated with public safety. While there is potential risk for inappropriate use of reclaimed water from the sources identified (e.g., World Health Organization publication titled “Guidelines for the safe use of wastewater, excreta and greywater - Volume 2: Wastewater use in agriculture”) to mitigate this risk, the project will provide technical assistance to farmers on the selection of appropriate "rain" crops (i.e. fodder and tree crops). In addition, the project will provide technical assistance to prevent misuse of reclaimed water, including building local capacity of farmers and the water user association.

28. ES5 - land acquisition and involuntary resettlement: The AE indicates that this is not applicable to the project. No land acquisitions are expected to occur in the implementation of the project activities and land tenure and any related environmental and social risks will be minimized by ensuring that all new facilities will be constructed on existing houses and on existing public facilities.

28. ES6 - biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural resources: The project will promote the use of locally available, indigenous crop varieties such as cultivars of barley and fruits, which are old varieties that have been tested and patented by the National Bureau of Plant Genetic Resources (NBPGR). This will ensure that the diversity of native species of flora and fauna into the target areas is minimized, if not avoided. Internal FAD (Generation) activities will be carried out in accordance with the principles of the FFS approach, and integrated pest management or other ecological pest management practices will be used where necessary.

28. GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and ES7: In its review, the AE has indicated that this is not applicable to the project. The AE furthermore indicates that should it become applicable through the course of the field level review, free, prior and informed consent will be applied.

### Implementation arrangements

A PMU needed in MoE will be established by the AE and will be comprised of technical specialists including a Social Inclusion and Gender Specialist and an Environmental Specialist. The PMU will oversee the implementation of the various component activities. UNDP, as the RE responsible for implementing the activities related to social inclusion and gender equality, will provide guidance on how to shape resilience plans, will apply its social and environmental screening procedures to identify potential social and environmental impacts associated with the proposed interventions and determine the appropriate level of assessment.

UNDP will conduct assessments as required in accordance with national laws and guidelines. UNDP will also ensure that all activities are carried out at the national and operational levels.

#### Stakeholder engagement and grievance redress mechanisms

During project scoping and preparation, a comprehensive effort was undertaken to solicit feedback from key stakeholders—including local communities and government agencies in the water and agriculture sectors—on the proposed resilience plans for each of the selected sub-areas. During these consultations, national priorities (i.e., agriculture, climate change, environment and rural and social development) were discussed with the aim of ensuring that they are aligned with broad inclusion of stakeholders. Engagement with technical experts was also conducted to better understand their views on the proposed resilience plans.

Proposed engagement activities to be implemented upon project approval will continue to focus on technical and national-level stakeholders as well as households and communities at the local level. The ESFM will develop a comprehensive stakeholder engagement plan for each of the local media in the selected sub-areas to develop appropriate approaches to engaging communities, including through community meetings, workshops, and other means. This will include rolling out appropriate grievance redress mechanisms; mainstreaming climate change adaptation practices and technologies; and producing training and learning materials.

The ESFM committee will engage with stakeholders and take into account their views on project implementation, in conjunction with the preparation of annual work plans and budgets. They will be provided with regular updates regarding progress towards achieving project objectives at the national and community levels. During these yearly consultations, the grievance redress mechanisms will also be reviewed.

The ESFM lays out a clear grievance redress structure for the project, involving various levels of complaint handling. The structure requires efforts to first address concerns at the level closest to where they arise. A formal complaints mechanism will be established at the field level for filing of formal complaints and guidance has been provided regarding how to file such complaints with the PMU and the AE’s Office of Inspector-General.

The PMU will be responsible for addressing grievances regarding ESFM standards, and for documenting and reporting on any issues that arise. If a grievance is not resolved at this level, it can be referred. If grievances are not resolved through consultations and mediation at the PMU level, a request for a compliance review can be filed with the AE’s Office of Inspector-General.

### Gender policy

The AE has provided a gender assessment and gender action plan (GAP), and therefore complies with the requirements of the Gender Policy of the fund.


---
**Page 105**

### The assessment demonstrates the commitment of Jordan towards gender equality and women’s empowerment.

Jordan ratified the revised Convention on the Elimination of all Forms of Discrimination Against Women in 2007. Equality before the law for all is guaranteed by Article 14 of the Constitution, which states that no one shall be discriminated against. However, Jordan's laws do not include an article addressing gender inequalities or criminalizing discrimination on the basis of sex. Women are subject to a number of discriminatory laws and practices, including those with restricted residency and citizenship rights for their children. The treatment of women as legal minors leaves women and girls under male legal guardianship on matters of marriage, divorce, alimony, and inheritance. Jordan has no specific law against domestic violence or sexual harassment punishable by law. The Law on Protection from Domestic Violence, No. 15 of 2017 protects women who are victims of domestic violence but does not provide for the rights of women entitled to inheritance at a level of half the share allotted to sons. Also, there are no labour laws that require equal pay for work of equal value or prohibit discrimination on grounds of sex.

According to the World Bank’s 2018 Gender Development Index (GDI), Jordan ranks among the lowest gender equality and highest gender gaps in the world (ranked 138 out of 152). According to the UNICEF report, “The State of Jordan’s Children,” published in 2017, for Jordan, 79 per cent of women between 18–64 years old have experienced domestic violence in their life. In 2018 the adult female literacy rate was 72.7 per cent; Jordan also has the lowest female labour force participation rate at only 35.5 per cent compared to a global average of 59.5 per cent and the lowest female agricultural landholding rate at just over 4 per cent, compared with an average of 18.6 per cent for all countries in the region.

The assessment provides an overview of the gender differences, gaps and dynamics in Jordan, with particular focus on the impact of climate change on women and their role in adapting to it. The assessment is based on a review of the National Strategy for Women in Jordan (NSWJ) 2015–2025, the 2018-2023 National Strategy for Women in Jordan and a newly formed gender unit within MWI that developed the Gender Action Plan (GAP) with the support of the International Union for Conservation of Nature. The main priority sectors of eCoCAP include water, energy, agriculture and land security, and waste reduction and management.

The assessment also provides an overview of the existing policies, strategies and frameworks for increasing women’s agency in development interventions for climate resilience through a participatory approach with national policies, gaps, research studies, donor initiatives and stakeholder consultations.


### The gender analysis recognizes that climate change, the depletion of natural resources and decreasing access to water and land are major challenges facing Jordan. These factors reduce time available to participate in decision making processes and income generating activities. Women’s vulnerability is further exacerbated by their lower levels of education disproportionately affected by climate change with unequal access to resources and assets, barriers to accessing information and services, and limited participation in decision-making.

Women play a critical role as agents of change in climate resilience and their full capacity and agency need to be unlocked through the development of gender-responsive policies, strategies, programs and adaptation practices; promoting networking and leadership opportunities; and promoting affirmative action, including setting quotas to create a level playing field for women’s increased access and participation. Women are also key actors in water management and household sanitation and waste management. Their roles in these areas are often undervalued or unrecognized as they are not considered important role in water management and household sanitation and waste management. Their roles in these areas are often undervalued or unrecognized as they are not considered important role in water management and household sanitation and waste management. Their roles in these areas are often undervalued or unrecognized as they are not considered important role in water management and household sanitation and waste management. Their roles in these areas are often undervalued or unrecognized as they are not considered important role in water management and household sanitation and waste management.

As women are the principal caretakers of animals and home gardens, and decision makers about household food security, their participation is critical to climate change adaptation strategies. The gender unit in the MWI has conducted a study on the status of women in the water sector. The study recommended developing a gender responsive planning and management system with

### Risks

#### Overall programme assessment (medium)

The proposal requests a grant of GEF USD 25 million for increased climate-resilient sustainable development in Jordan. The project consists of three components addressing climate change adaptation and mitigation through improved water management, enhanced institutional capacity at various levels. Co-financing is to be provided by the Government of Jordan (USD 4.2 million), FAO (in-kind USD 1 million) and GIZ (in-kind USD 0.6 million and grant USD 350 thousand).

#### Accredited entity/executing entity capable to execute the current programme (medium)

FAO will serve both as the accredited entity, responsible for supervising the project, and as one of the executing entities for the project. FAO has a track record in implementing agricultural and climate resilience projects and has presence in Jordan. In Jordan, FAO has worked with the Government on several initiatives related to agriculture and natural production. FAO will establish a PMU to coordinate the activities of the executing entities.

UNDP will be an co-executing entity with the Government of Jordan for several activities under component 1 of the programme. UNDP has an extensive track record in implementing


---
**Page 107**
---

projects and has considerable experience in working with MoE; it has also supported a number of entrepreneurial policy reform actions in Jordan.

47. The Hashemite Kingdom of Jordan will be an executing entity of the programme through IWMI, MoA and MoE, with MoE acting as Jordan's national designated authority for GCF. For strategic guidance on the implementation of the programme, the PMF will be established by ministries, as its steering committee. The PMF established by FAO will have a seat in MoE and be supported by FAO and UNDP to provide technical assistance and advice.

### 4.3.2 Project specific risks (medium)

**60. Political and governance risk:** The AE identified political and governance matters as risks for project execution with medium probability. Policy changes such as strict enforcement of existing policies or changes in water efficiency policies could be sensitive to political risk. Reassurance can be derived from the commitment of MoE to implement the programme irrespective of UNDP and FAO in the host country.

**61. Commitment risk:** Some types of household rainwater systems are exposed to construction and maintenance risks. Reassurance can be derived from the commitment of FAO to implement only after technical clearance and individual feasibility assessments.

* Project viability: The AE identified that a significant upgrade of climate-resilient practices would reduce the impact of the programme. Reassurance is derived from (i) co-financing commitment for women to encourage participation and (ii) expected financial benefits to net beneficiaries.

**63. The AE notes that expected contribution from the Government could be jeopardized due to budget constraints.**

### 4.3.1 Project viability and concessionality

The project also demonstrated an aggregate rate of return of 25.1 per cent including benefit-cost ratio (BCR) of 0.86, aggregating economic incremental benefits to be compared with total project costs. The models show positive net present values and financial internal rates of return for all the scenarios considered in this proposal. The AE notes that expected increases in household income are estimated between 15 per cent for the average total household size (2.4) and 30 per cent for a single-person household. For example, 1 per cent eligibility criteria have different amounts of support per target group to target concessionality to more vulnerable households.

### GCF's portfolio concentration risk (low risk):

In case of approval, the impact of this proposal on the GCF portfolio concentration in terms of financial size and target proposal is not material.

### Compliance

Jordan is not subject to United Nations Security Council resolutions. FAO, as the AE, confirmed that it implements/executes all of its projects in full compliance with the United Nations Norms and Principles.

FAO conducted an assessment of risks for money-laundering, terrorist financing and prohibited practices, and considers such risks to be very marginal. Procurement and many other activities involving the expenditure of GCF funds will be managed by FAO and UNDP directly.

### 4.5 Results monitoring and reporting

The stated outcomes of the theory of change are defined in a manner that is clearly supportive of meeting the ultimate project goal. However, the AU is guided to reformulate the "TC" logic model to include the following: (i) improved access to water resources; (ii) farmers' knowledge of water saving and conserving technologies and awareness of climate adaptive practices; (iii) increased capacity for climate change adaptation in the farming community; (iv) information dissemination and policy development is improved THEN the adaptive capacity and resilience of vulnerable communities will be strengthened BECAUSE the exposure to climate change risks is reduced.

At the core indicator level, the logical framework has been designed with relevant details. It includes indicators that are aligned with the AU's monitoring and evaluation frameworks. This includes reporting on the appropriate core indicator for adaptation (number of direct and indirect beneficiaries disaggregated by sex).

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP155

## Proposal name: Building resilience to cope with climate change in Jordan through improving water use efficiency in the agriculture sector (EWS)

### Accredited entity: Food and Agricultural Organization of the United Nations (FAO)

### Country/Region: Hashemite Kingdom of Jordan

### Project/programme size: Small

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1. Impact potential Scale: High

#### 1.1 Adaptation impact

##### 1.1.3 Adaptation impact

The project seeks to enhance the climate resiliency of the population of four targeted governorates in the Dead Sea basin (Karak Mts., Madaba and Tafila) by improving their capacity to cope with water scarcity, droughts, floods and other extreme weather events caused by climate vulnerability and is exacerbated by climate change. This would be achieved through investments in rainwater harvesting systems, irrigation infrastructure, and improved agricultural practices.

2. Component 1 focuses on increasing water supply through: (1) the installation of rainwater harvesting (RWH) systems in 400 public buildings and 7500 households; (2) the construction of a new dam with an installed capacity of 3 million cubic metres for irrigation of the fodder; and (3) the design of an investment portfolio, for inter alia, infrastructure development, water supply, sanitation, drainage, flood control, area works, weirs and delay action dams, storage ponds and water diversion structures.

As described in the proposal, "the siting of the tanks for RWH was done using a 'supply split' approach. The volume of each tank is calculated as the greatest excess of water over and above consumption, based on expected monthly rainfall (as originally presented by the accredited entity (AE) in Annex 2). The AE responded with an adequate water storage/mixage balance based on monthly rainfall data to the satisfaction of the independent Technical Advisory Panel. This will contribute significantly towards climate resilience through improved access to water and efficient water use at the household level."


---

## To enhance capacities to use reclaimed wastewater for the irrigation of fodder crops, the project selected the Madaba, Karak and Tafila wastewater treatment plants where storage capacity is limited and water harvesting is not feasible.

The calculation of additional storage capacity was based on on-site land availability and surplus wastewater from the existing treatment plants. The total available storage capacity is 150 million cubic meters. With water harvesting, the supply side here is also the limiting variable. There are unused capacities in the Madaba and Karak wastewater treatment plants which are 1.5 to 2 times greater than what is currently under cultivation.

### Given that secondary wastewater treatment plants using activated sludge and trickling filter technologies have been constructed at all three sites, there was no need for additional capacity. About the prevention and mitigation of contamination from nematodes while reusing treated wastewater in irrigation systems, it should be noted that the presence of nematode eggs appears to be rare* and provided laboratory data for the period 2019-2024, plus a letter from the Ministry of Water and Irrigation that supported this statement. In addition, there is no evidence of any contamination in the water supply systems of identical tanks and their construction follows the Jordanian and World Health Organization standards to reduce the risks of contamination.

The central objective of Component 1 is the development of reclaimed water tanks and their management and maintenance will be secured at start up*.

### Activity 1.1.3 consists of the development of a portfolio for infrastructure investments for the rehabilitation of existing irrigation systems, construction of new dams and delay action dams, storage ponds and water diversion structures.

**Component 2 is basically focused on capacity-building and communication to enhance livelihoods in rural areas through training farmers trained by farmer field schools and field days in climate-resilient agricultural techniques, inter alia, the introduction of seed varieties that are more tolerant to drought conditions, improved irrigation technologies, water harvesting soft infrastructure (gully plugs, contour bunds), conservation agriculture practices, and agroforestry. The project will also provide training for farmers who would be trained and certified in state-of-the-art techniques for climate adaptive agriculture, agro-horticulture, and livestock production. In addition to the above, the project includes adaptation advocacy. To increase the use of climate information in decision-making, the project would develop smart applications to be used by the Ministry of Agriculture, the Ministry of Environment and Energy, and other relevant stakeholders. The project will also provide information on climate change adaptation and weather forecasts. It is expected that this component will benefit 100 farmers per year with a total area of approximately 250,000 hectares.*

The principal identified the main institutional barriers which delay climate change adaptation in the water and agriculture sector in Jordan as the lack of coherence of national policies and the lack of technical skills and resources. Component 3 would promote the mainstreaming of climate change adaptation into all relevant sectors by integrating it directly into the national policy and educational, administrative, economic and social frameworks.

10. Policy and the regulatory framework would be assured and updated to promote the use of drought-tolerant grains, water-efficient agricultural techniques, RWII systems for rooftops in rural areas and water-saving devices. This would be sought through, inter alia, the interpretation of existing laws and regulations.

12. The independent TAP requesting the AE to substantiate the claim made in the funding proposal that the use of water saving devices would reduce water use by 30 per cent. In response, the AE provided a technical note explaining why it is not possible for this activity to be executed by beneficiaries with their own resources and that it is not included as co-financing.

13. Activity 3.1.1 includes the provision of technical assistance to improve the monitoring protocols of farms irrigated with reclaimed water.

14. Activity 3.2.1 includes the provision of technical assistance to stakeholders to identify and initiate the necessary policy reforms related to water quality standards for the use of reclaimed water for irrigation, monitoring protocols for farms irrigated with reclaimed water, and other measures that would promote the adoption of this practice in the planning and designing of new wastewater treatment plants or the expansion of the existing ones.

15. The curricula of vocational schools (e.g., masonry, plumbing and agriculture) and of specialized universities (e.g., agriculture, architecture and water engineering) would be updated to include knowledge on climate change adaptation, principles and elements of adaptive water management and climate-adaptive technologies, principles and elements of RWII irrigation systems, and the use of reclaimed water for agricultural purposes in construction. Also, 6,480 people from the private sector, civil society and local institutions would receive training on the benefits of using reclaimed water for agriculture and to address barriers that hinder climate change adaptation, and on specific water-saving actions and climate-adaptive technologies. In addition, 120 persons (including 30 women) involved in the project areas and experienced in the water and agriculture sectors*would receive training on introduced climate adaptive technologies and practices in agriculture and water use.

16. Knowledge would be shared with farmers through community dialogues and events under component I (RWII and reclaimed water) and people who would receive capacity-building activities under component II (climate change adaptation). The target audience for beneficiaries would be the 15,000 people sensitized to climate-adaptive measures through Community Dialogues in each governorate and the Climate-Wise Women’s Forum, and the almost 13,000 farmers who would receive training on water-saving devices and upgrades in the agriculture and water sectors.*

### Innovation

1.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: Medium

1.3.3 Innovation

17. The promotion of climate-resilient agricultural techniques, wastewater reuse and RWII combined with water-saving devices, are new to Jordan. As a matter of fact, the calculations of the benefits of using reclaimed water for agriculture in Jordan and its associated costs are based on previous experiences in Jordan. However, conservation agriculture, wicking beds, and aquaponics and hydroponics, as well as the development of short applications for the community dialogues on climate change adaptation and weather forecasts, can be considered an innovative aspect of this funding proposal.

*Minimum of two per governorate.

### Potential for knowledge and learning

In capacity-building activities are present in all project components. The installation of RWH systems will be complemented by awareness-raising and orientations on water conservation at the household level, for municipal staff in public buildings and children and teachers in schools. Component 2 includes updating the curriculum to include sustainable agricultural practices, mainly through training activities. Component 3 includes updating the curriculum to include climate change adaptation and related topics and providing capacity-building to 4,400 people from the rural sector, civil society and local institutions.

#### Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment

Regarding the storage and distribution infrastructure for reclaimed water, the Ministry of Water and Irrigation and the Water Authority of Jordan signed a formal agreement assuming responsibility for the operations and maintenance (O&M) costs during and after the project, with the aim of ensuring sustainability. The O&M responsibilities will be divided between existing and new Water User Associations regarding O&M at the field level and the costing and rational use of reclaimed water.

The RWH measures in public buildings would be maintained by the agency responsible for the O&M of each building, and individual households would maintain their own RWH structures. The maintenance costs are expected to be low because they will be covered by O&M. Household RWH systems have low maintenance costs and can be expected to be affordable even for low-income families.

#### Contribution to the regulatory framework and policies

Activity 3.1.1 is dedicated to advancing the policy and regulatory framework to incentivize the adoption of sustainable agricultural practices and the use of RWH systems and water-saving devices, and would support stakeholders in enhancing standards for the monitoring and evaluation of projects with reclaimed water.

### Scalability and replicability

The main characteristic that contributes to the upscaling potential of the project is its strong emphasis on capacity-building, awareness-raising and institutional strengthening, and the incorporation of climate change in the current agricultural practices and policies. The companies involved in the market for agricultural inputs and equipment will be invited to participate in the field farming schools, field days, trainings, workshops and conferences on introduced techniques and practices, and on awareness-raising and communication campaigns and events.

### Sustainable development potential

The project will contribute to a number of Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), for example SDG 2 (Zero hunger); SDG 3 (Good health and well-being); SDG 5 (Gender equality); SDG 6 (Clean water and sanitation) and SDG 13 (Climate action), among other SDGs.

# Environmental co-benefits

25. The increase in the use of reclaimed water and rainwater would take pressure off sources of surface water, benefiting the aquatic ecosystems that depend on them. The use of reclaimed water would decrease the flow of treated wastewater that is currently being discharged into the Dead Sea. In addition, the implementation of climate-smart agriculture, which would be implemented on over 10,000 hectares, would have positive effects on soil quality.

# Social co-benefits

26. The implementation of climate-resilient agricultural practices and the increased availability for small-homestead agricultural activities and fodder production would improve food security and, consequently, the health status of the population.

# Economic co-benefits

27. Economic co-benefits include savings in water that, without this project, would be purchased from Jordanian suppliers, reduced consumption of water through the use of water-saving devices, and incremental revenues for farmers using reclaimed water for fodder irrigation.

# Gender-sensitive development impact

28. Capacity-building in climate-resilient agriculture is strongly focused on empowering women. Activity 2.1.3 would create a group of 400 "climate-wise women" to act as agents of change for their communities, through training and awareness-raising activities at community dialogues and household visits to raise awareness of the importance of the role that women can have in climate-resilient agriculture. The project will also provide information on how women are affected by climate change phenomena, the possible impacts on their lives and adaptation measures, such as water-saving devices and practices at the domestic level, simple greenhouses, drip irrigation systems, and the production and use of growbags.

## Needs of the recipient

### Climate rationale

25. The original funding proposal states that, according to Jordan's Third National Communication (TNC) to the UNFCCC, annual maximum and minimum temperatures in the country have increased since 1960 by 0.3–1.8°C per decade, with a significant increase of up to 4.5°C for the maximum temperature. Since 1900, annual precipitation decreased at most meteorological stations, and according to the International Centre for Water Resources Research (ICWRR), the average rainfall over the Dead Sea basin decreased by up to 20 per cent in the period 1931–2016. However, the source data for these conclusions are limited to a few meteorological stations. The annual maximum precipitation and annual mean maximum and minimum temperatures for the period 1979–2019 present data from the European Centre for Medium-Range Weather Forecasts (ECMWF) ERA5 dataset. These data show that the average temperature in Jordan has increased, while climate change is already affecting the project area. Questioned by the independent TAP about the sources of data related to climate change, the Ministry of Environment confirmed that the main source of data related to climate change is the TNC, which is based on local records from 19 meteorological stations, and that 15 of these stations showed downward trends in

* Feasibility study: page 21–22.


---
**Page 118**
---

annual precipitation and increasing trends in both minimum and maximum temperatures (reference period 1980–2010). The proponent submitted historical data from the five relevant and existing meteorological stations of Karak, Madaba, Shushan, Tafalla and Wadi Musa, showing a general increase in temperature. The proponent explained that the ECMWF ERA5 dataset is produced by the European Climate Change Assessment Programme using data from both satellite sensors and to–o–site data, including meteorological stations.

30 Without data on temporal distribution, mean annual values are insufficient to assess the real impact of climate change on water resources in Jordan. The proponent submitted a request from the independent TAP that the PEA presented data on the observed variation of monthly temperatures.

15 The source of the precipitation and temperature projections are not clear; nor is whether the models were validated against locally recorded historical data. Questioned by the independent TAP, the proponent explained that the models used for the precipitation and temperature projection models that were officially communicated by the country in the TNC. These models are based on the output of the climate change scenarios developed at the meteorological stations (19 total). Dynamic downscaling based on the Coordinated Regional Climate Downscaling Experiment (CORDEX) system has been used in the by the TNC for the projections utilized within the baseline and future scenarios. The CORDEX system consists of 20 regional climate models with 56-kilometre resolution were crossed throughout the country. Nine different GCMs coupled with two downscaling methods were used to generate the ensemble of projections. These are based on reference historical data (1980–2010). Three time horizons were selected: 2020-2050, 2040-2070, and 2070-2100. The TNC used the ensemble of projections generated by the models communicated by the country – in available in the TNC (pages 118 to 120). These models are largely used by Jordanian authorities for climate change adaptation planning and decision-making, including the “climate change scenario for the project”. In a second set of responses, the AE indicated that: “the nine selected models were further processed for the sensitivity analysis, which was conducted and debated using local meteorological stations and climatic weather stations. Debiasing was achieved using (1) the delta method, and (2) the quantile–quantile method (Meteo-France and French Meteorological Service). The reference period used in the ensemble of projections [3] 1980-2010 was used as a reference period and was centred on the year 1995; [2020-2050 was used for near-term projections, and was centred on the year 2040; [2070-2100] was used for far future projections, and was centred on the year 2060. The TNC has not provided any information to support the statements made satisfy the climate rationale requirements of the independent TAP.

### Vulnerability of the country, vulnerable groups and gender aspects

* The households that benefit from the IFAD project were expected to contribute part of the cost of the project, except for the most vulnerable groups, which would be financed 100 per cent by the project. The proposal identifies the most vulnerable groups as women-headed households with no land or livestock; those living in urban areas and working in smallholdings and gardens, and those dependent entirely on agriculture having up to one hectare of land with the possibility of increasing their holdings to two hectares within five years. A household with four or more than six dependents would be required to pay 30 per cent of the total, and other households would have to pay 65 per cent of the cost.

* The number of beneficiaries targeted by the project area is estimated at 40,000 with a total of about 24,000 rural people living in the four targeted governorates. There are about 20 regions in Jordan with a poverty rate of more than 25 per cent, consisting of 134 communities.

* Institut National de la Recherche Agronomique


---
**Page 119**
---

with a population of about half a million citizens. Three areas of poverty are distributed in 9 of the 12 governorates in Jordan. The governorate of Ma'an has six of these regions, the governorate of Karak has three, and the governorate of Tafith has one.**10**

The high vulnerability of households that depend on agriculture is because a high proportion of them are located in areas with poor soil quality.

With regard to water service, 94 per cent of the population in Jordan has access to safe drinking water. However, only about half of these people have access to sanitation. About 30 per cent of households are connected to the public sewer system. Compliance with the microbiological indicators of the World Health Organization for the water supplied in the entire country was 97.5 per cent.

About 90 per cent of the reclaimed wastewater produced is reused directly by farmers or indirectly through mixing with rainwater and surface water in dams.


### Economic and social development

As a result of the conflicts in neighbouring Syria, which are Jordan's main trading partners, the export-oriented economy has worsened and the fiscal deficit rose. The gross domestic product growth, which averaged 6.4 per cent during 2000–2005, fell below 2.5 per cent over 2010–2013. According to World Bank estimates based on purchasing power parity (PPP) international standards, the poverty rate in 2017 was estimated to be 15.7 per cent. The refugee crisis adds pressure on public services and infrastructure, including education, health care, housing, water and the labour market. The unemployment rate is estimated to have reached 19.2 per cent in 2013, and youth unemployment is reported to be as high as 60 per cent.

The economic development of each targeted governorate is briefly described in the feasibility study.


#### Absence of alternative sources of financing

Resources to build resilience to climate change are very limited. The current context shows high external pressures and very high public debt (International Monetary Fund Press Release No. 14/79, 2014). Nevertheless, there has been some progress in the area of investment.

#### Need for strengthening institutions and implementation capacity

Weak institutional capacity is one of the main barriers to climate adaptation identified in the proposal and would be addressed through activity 3.1.1.


### Country ownership Scale: High

#### Alignment with the national strategy and policies

The project is aligned with Jordan's National Green Growth Plan (2017), which stresses the importance of the efficient use of water resources, knowledge transfer, and the introduction of drought-tolerant varieties, which are at the objectives of the National Climate Change Policy (2013–2020), the National Adaptation Plan (2020) and the intended nationally determined contribution (2015).

### Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: Medium

#### 1.6 Cost-effectiveness and efficiency

As shown in the response of FAO to the independent TAP questions, **17** the delivery system for the reclaimed water (i.e., pipelines) makes up more than 70 per cent of the total investment cost. The costs associated with the construction of these systems are not presented in the proposal, and for this reason it is not possible to assess the adequacy of the budget for this activity.

The estimated annual incremental income from households in the proposed TAP system in households in the range of normally expected costs.

As per the project budget, the new storage and distribution infrastructure for reclaimed water that would be used by 106,582 households (968 people) would have an annual O&M cost of USD 15,300. If the Ministry of Water and Irrigation was to design a tariff structure to cover all O&M costs, this would result in an average monthly water bill for each household of USD 17.90 per month. As a point of comparison, these farmers are expected to generate an incremental income from their farm activities of USD 260 per year.

The funding proposal indicates that each farmer that uses reclaimed water for irrigation would generate an incremental revenue of USD 620 per year, and that the water utility would


---
**Page 121**
---

save USD 10,000 per year due to the avoided cost of tertiary treatment of the treated wastewater that the project would use for irrigation. However, the three selected wastewater treatment plants have secondary treatment, and there is no mention of wastewater treatment plants with tertiary treatment in the funding proposal. Therefore, there is no reason to count avoided tertiary treatment as a saving.

In the opinion of the independent TAP, to meet Jordan’s new requirements for water reuse projects, Crefco (e.g., soddy) should be included in the project scope. Thereby use it to calculate the cost and benefits of the projects if flawed. However, based on the experience of numerous wastewater reuse systems elsewhere, the independent TAP conclusion is that no additional costs are incurred by a country with such levels of water stress, always pays back if adequately implemented.

### 1.2.1 Amount of co-financing

50 total project costs and are estimated to be USD 37.4 million. GCF would provide USD 25 million (67% of the total project cost) and the Government of Jordan is committed to providing USD 6.2 million (19 per cent). FAO and UNDP would co-finance USD 2.06 million (1 million for each country, respectively, representing 6 per cent of total costs). The beneficiaries are expected to provide USD 4.6 million for investments at the household level.

### 1.2.3 Financial viability

The proposal estimates that households that receive RWH systems would save USD 127 per year (assuming a household would save 30 m³ of water per year) due to the implementation of wastewater reuse systems. The savings are as follows: private sector buildings would save because they currently purchase from tankers, and the public buildings would save between USD 363 and USD 585 due to reduced use of purchased water (assuming savings of USD 0.17 per m³).

# Introduction

1. The objective of the gender assessment is to facilitate and inform gender mainstreaming in the Full Funding Proposal to the Green Climate Fund (GCF) for the project “Jordan Adaptation to Climate Change in Jordan”. The assessment aims to provide an overview of the gender situation and dynamics in Jordan, with particular focus on women’s agency in climate change adaptation. It seeks to identify key gender issues in climate change adaptation and strategies for increasing women's agency in development interventions for climate resilience through a more inclusive approach that takes into account the needs of women and other stakeholders consultation. The findings of the assessment have been used to make the project more gender-sensitive, with a focus on increasing women’s agency and enhancing women’s agency to deal with climate risks. Part I of the report provides an overview of the gender situation in Jordan and Part II provides a Gender Action Plan and the gender mainstreaming strategy.

2. A number of key interviews were conducted with experts (KEIs) to understand how climate change was impacting the agriculture sector and its gender assessment aspects, including women’s access to resources and assets. The interviewees included: Ministry of Agriculture (MoA), National Agriculture Research Center (NARC), Gender Unit in the MoA, Jordanian Women’s Union (JWU), Jordan Humanitarian Fund (JOHUD), Department of Statistics (DoS), Partners for Good, International Union for Conservation of Nature (IUCN), UN Women, Jordan River Foundation (JRF). The team also conducted focus group discussions with women and men in the villages including both Syrian Refugees and host communities to understand their perceptions on climate change and its impact on their livelihoods. The key findings from these interviews include the following: women are disproportionately affected by climate change with unequal access to resources and assets, barriers to decision-making and lack of information about climate change, the need for change in climate resilience and their full capacity and agency needs to be unlocked through gender-sensitive approaches that take into account the different roles of men and women, building women’s knowledge of climate change and adaptation practices, and by the provision of networking and leadership opportunities; affirmative action, including quotas, can help increase women's representation. Women need increased access and control over resources; interventions have to be customized to women’s needs and capacities in order for them to be able to participate and to be able to access and benefit from them; and women in leadership / decision-making positions have a powerful impact both for opening pathways for other women as well as increasing women's access to resources.

## Community Consultations

3. Two consultations were held in the target governorates of Madaba and Karak, near the Jordan River, and 7 men with the support of UN Women Coalition – JOHUD. Women and men mostly commented on how the difference in temperatures had affected their crops and livelihoods, the water shortage and its effect on agriculture and water for domestic purposes. Water shortage had increased problems with soil,
