4. The design team visited all four project governorates in February 2020 and had dedicated meetings with local extension agents in the Directorate of Agriculture in the project area and met with the technical staff of NARC research centres as well as the technicians who are responsible for training farmers, both men and women. The Director General of the Extension Department also helped organize meetings with her staff in the project governorates to understand how women could be involved in the project. The team also visited the pilot sites where the project will be implemented for both men and women to learn from their experience of implementing the IFAID financed projects. The team also met with the Jordanian Association of Women Engineers, with the Association of Agriculture Engineers to identify and train women agricultural engineers for the North of the country in the REESEP project, thus building a cadre of women engineers who can be involved in the project. The team also met with IFS facilitators and developed curricula for the identified target crops in cooperation with FAO on the ground. The technologies that will be used to train farmers and the technologies the project will be promoting have already been tried and tested by NARC.

Part 1: Overview of Jordan's Gender Profile

5. Women constitute about 47.1 percent of the total population of Jordan. Although Jordan’s Human Development Index places it in the high human development category, its score on the Gender Development Index (GDI), 147 out of 168, is low and puts Jordan at a lower rank than other countries with similar levels of health knowledge and living standards. The 2020 Global Gender Gap report ranks Jordan 138 out of 153 countries* based on indicators of economic participation and opportunity, educational attainment, political empowerment, and health and survival. Women in Politics 2019 map ranked Jordan 132 out of 193 countries, mapping women’s representation at the national level. The first gender equality law was achieved through the gender quota system adopted in 2003, where later revisions to the system increased the allocated quotas for women from 6 to 15 parliamentary candidates.

6. The Gender Development Index (GDI) is a composite index that measures reproductive health, empowerment and labour market participation, and is a measure of the human development cost stemming from gender inequality; has decreased between 2009 and 2018 by 34 percent, indicating that Jordan has made progress towards closing the gender gap*. Jordan ratified the revised Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) in 2007. The CEDAW requires all signatories to eliminate discrimination against women in all areas of economic and social life. In addition, it requires that “States Parties shall take all appropriate measures to ensure full development of the personality and talents of every child with a view to his or her physical, mental, moral and social development.” In order to ensure, on a basis of equality of men and women, that they participate in and


---
**Page 130**
---

7. Health: The World Health Organization (WHO) categorizes Jordan as a country in early nutrition transition, where widespread micronutrient deficiencies and moderate levels of stunting are observed among children under five years old. The prevalence of children with moderate overweight and obesity is **high**. Chronic malnutrition among Jordanian children is relatively low; 10 percent severely underweight, 23 percent moderately underweight, 2 percent severely overnourished, 3 percent severely underweight, and 4 percent overweight. Infant and under-5 mortality rates in the past five years are 17 per 1,000 live births on a population of one million people. The infant mortality rate has decreased from 98 deaths per 1,000 live births in 1990 to 19 deaths per 1,000 live births in 2017. The neonatal mortality rate has also decreased significantly, from 39 deaths per 1,000 live births in 1990 to 19 deaths per 1,000 live births in 2017. This means that the neonatal mortality rate is now less than half of what it was in 1990 and about one-third times the post-neonatal rate. The perinatal mortality rate is 13 per 1,000 pregnancies.

Eighty-six percent of Jordanian children aged 12-23 months are fully immunized. Breastfeeding rates have increased significantly over time; currently, 95 percent of mothers breastfeed for at least six months or longer and half of children breastfed for about 12 months or longer. Overall, only one-third of children age 6–23 months receive adequate complementary feeding practices, while less than a third are receiving optimal complementary feeding practices. Thirty-two percent of children aged 5-9 months are anemic; 21 per cent are mildly anemic, 10.5 percent are moderately anemic, and less than 1 percent are severely anemic.

8. An alarming 54 percent of women are overweight or obese. Forty-three percent of women aged 15–49 are obese, 36 percent are mildly anemic, 6.3 percent are moderately anemic, and 0.7 percent are severely anemic. Ninety-eight percent of all women (98 percent) received antenatal care from medically trained personnel; the majority of women (85 percent) received at least four antenatal care visits during their pregnancy. Ninety-seven percent of women make seven or more antenatal care visits during their entire pregnancy. Twenty-eight percent of mothers with a birth in the five years preceding the survey were delivered by cesarean section, and 90 percent of births occurred at home (86 percent) in the five years preceding the survey were delivered in a medical facility. There is an increase in the percentage of women who received postnatal care from a doctor, nurse, or midwife within the first critical two days after a delivery. Between 2012 and 2017-18, the total fertility rate fell from 3.5 to 2.7 children per woman.

The prevalence of maternal mortality has decreased relatively stable, as 3.5 to 3.8 children per woman during the decade between the 2002 and 2012 surveys.


---
**Page 131**
---

9. **Gender-based Violence (GBV):** According to the UNFPA scorecard for GBV in Jordan, it is recorded that 79 percent of women between 18-44 years old have experienced domestic violence in their life, with 14 percent of ever-partnered women aged 15-49 years having experienced sexual violence and/or rape by their partner within the last year. The percentage of women who have experienced emotional abuse is higher than that for physical or sexual abuse. The rural/urban gradient governs the percentage of ever-married women who have experienced emotional abuse, with 20 percent in Amman, 18 percent in Zarqa, 35 percent in Irbid, Madaba 24 percent, Karak 10 percent, Tafila 15 percent Maan 29 percent. Furthermore, between 15 and 20 women are victims of honour killings every year, on average.

10. **Education:** In 2018 adult female literacy rate was 7.2 percent, whereas it was only 3.1 percent for adult men. There is a positive correlation between education and economic empowerment. Women with secondary or higher education have a greater chance of being employed in the public sector than those who are illiterate or have lower degree. Twenty-two per cent of unemployed women are educated and hold an intermediate or higher qualification.

11. **Unemployment:** Unemployment rates in Jordan have remained between 12 and 14 percent, approximately 11 percent for males and 20 percent for women, for over a decade. The female unemployment rate has increased from 18 to 25 percent since 2017, an increase by 0.3 percentage points since 2018. Two groups that are particularly affected are women and youth. There is not a strong bivariate link between labour force participation rates of women and the size of the economy or non-participation and the size of the public sector.

12. **Women's economic participation:** Jordan has the lowest female labour force participation rate in the MENA region, at 30 percent compared to an average of 54 percent for men. Women face particular obstacles and disincentives to economic participation. Within the MENA region, Jordan’s female labour force participation rate is below the regional average of 42 per cent and is only 25 percent. In 2017 the female labour force participation rate in Jordan was about a fifth of that of males (17.7 percent versus 59.4 percent). A World Bank study shows a 12.3 percent increase in women’s employment for every one percentage point increase in the private sector. The average monthly wage for men is JD 403 [USD$ 568], compared to JD 350 [USD $50] for women. It is worth noting that for decision making professions, this discrepancy was much higher; for example female legislators, senior


---
**Page 132**
---

officials and managers are paid about 32 percent less than men.15

13. Despite many efforts directed toward enhancing women's role in the society and in the economy, there has been little progress in women's economic participation. Employer preferences for female employees are often based on stereotypes about women's abilities to perform certain jobs or tasks. This is reflected in hiring practices that limit women's access to certain job positions. Women are also underrepresented in decision-making positions at businesses and organizations. The highest gender gap is evident in the agricultural sector where women make up 65 percent of farm labourers. However, the percentage of agricultural holdings headed by women has declined from 30 per cent in 1984 to only 27 per cent in 2014. Women households headed by women own agricultural land and 20 per cent own livestock. Instead, a majority of households headed by men own land and 36 percent of them own livestock.16

14. Gender Norms Inhibiting Women's Economic Participation: A World Bank study17 indicates that the largest share of women (58%) are constrained in their ability to participate in economic activities because they are expected to take care of the home and almost two-thirds of non-working women would like to work. Women's preferences and personal beliefs are not a major obstacle to participation, therefore there are no significant barriers to women's economic participation.

15. The Way Forward: Gender norms may be an important barrier for women working in mixed-sex environments pose certain barriers for women. This may be partially attributed to fears about harassment, as 44 percent of participants in the study believe that working women are exposing themselves to harassment. These beliefs may not


---
**Page 133**
---

only restrict the types of jobs women can take up (i.e., jobs in mixed-sex workplaces), but also give rise to additional concerns about personal safety and reputation when deciding whether to work. Many working women (58 percent) believe that it is okay for women to work in environments where most other employees are men.

16. Inequalities in the division of household chores and childcare responsibilities are a major concern, with most respondents reporting that they share these tasks equally with their partners. However, some respondents reported that their partners expected them to take on more domestic duties than themselves. Most respondents also reported that their partners expected them to take care of all of the household chores (cooking, cleaning, caring for children), while they, in turn, expected their partners to take care of childcare and other responsibilities.

17. In many cases, women are unable to combine work with family life because of lack of childcare or lack of flexibility in working conditions, unavailability of part-time work, hiring and wage discrimination, limited job growth, lack of adequate public services (e.g., child care), and the fact that most jobs are located far from home. Women’s economic participation is also hindered by a lack of childcare, lack of flexibility in working conditions, unavailability of part-time work, hiring and wage discrimination, limited job growth, lack of adequate public services (e.g., child care), and the fact that most jobs are located far from home.

18. Women’s economic participation is also hindered by a lack of childcare, lack of flexibility in working conditions, unavailability of part-time work, hiring and wage discrimination, limited job growth, lack of adequate public services (e.g., child care), and the fact that most jobs are located far from home. A key driver for women’s employment in Jordan is financial necessity. The majority of respondents indicated that they would consider working or joining the workforce, the majority of participants indicated that the high cost of living would force them to find a job.

19. There have been some improvements in women's rights and gender equality since 2007, such as Article 256 to 186 of the Penal Code which criminalizes rape, stating that rape outside of marriage is punishable by 15 years in prison. Article 302 and 308 under the Penal Code now criminalize sexual harassment and sexual assault, respectively. In 2017 Article 98 of the Penal Code was amended, providing reduction of penalties for women who are raped or sexually assaulted if they report their assailant. The Law on Protection from Domestic Violence, No. 15 of 2017 protects women against domestic violence; however it does not include a definition of what constitutes domestic violence.

20. Jordanian laws addressed several issues related to women such as maternity leave, breast feeding leave, providing a suitable place for children less than four years,

prohibiting the termination of jobs for women who are 8 months pregnant and above or during maternity leaves, prohibiting assigning tasks that are dangerous or threatening to women's health and equal treatment of men and women, with no discrimination. However, these provisions have not been sufficient for improving the participation of women in decision-making processes at all levels of government and society. There are no explicit statements in Labor Law that require equal pay for men and women.

21. Women’s political participation and representation: Women were granted the right to vote and stand as candidates in elections for municipal councils, town councils, and village councils in 1974 and 1982 respectively (CEDAW, 1997). To ensure the political participation of women, the Jordanian government took several proactive action and amended the Electoral Law in 2003 to include a quota for women. [2]

National Strategies and Policies for Gender Equality

22. The Kingdom of Jordan has introduced policies and strategies to mainstream gender more effectively since 1987 with the introduction of its National Strategy for Women in Jordan, aims to end “all forms of gender-based violence and discrimination” and is developed through a participatory process involving women’s groups. It highlights rights potential with equal opportunities, to ensure sustainable development for the Kingdom. Furthermore, the King Hussein Foundation Information supported the Jordanian National Commission for Women (JNCW) in developing a set of guidelines for mainstreaming gender and highlighting gender priorities related to the Jordan 2025 Vision. [3] The JNCW also developed a national action plan on women’s rights for women; promotion of positive cultural practices, traditions, and mindsets that promote respect for human rights values and advancement for women; amending or enacting laws and regulations in accordance with international standards against any discrimination in any form and ensuring that women and vulnerable citizens are protected from violence, abuse and exploitation and are supported to enjoy all their rights as Jordanian citizens. [3]

23. Jordan has a host of national strategies to address the impacts of climate change, as well as to be ever more resilient to its effects. The importance of women’s participation and active role in the adoption to these changes is highlighted: National Strategy and Action Plan to Combat Desertification 2015-2020, the National Water Strategy 2016-2035, the National Climate Change Strategy 2016-2025. The Plan to Combat Desertification states that the revised plan will include a gender perspective and will focus on increasing water availability; decreasing food security in particular and increasing poverty on women, as a result of deforestation[4]. The National Water Strategy studies the challenging situations in relation to water resources and the environment and highlights the importance of gender mainstreaming in the water sector. It recognises the crucial role women play at all production stages and in decision-making processes for the protection of the environment. An objective of the plan is to include women in the decision-making processes and in service delivery implementation as a measure to enhance women’s

24. Furthermore, in the National Strategy for Women in Jordan (NSW 2020-2025), the conservation of the environment constitutes a sub-section with specific objectives and activities. The strategy aims at ensuring that the conservation of the environment and its development must be enhanced. Mitigating for volatile changes to the climate has a set of proposed measures, including integration of existing knowledge and experience in water resources management into national natural resources; supporting existing initiatives of recycling, facilitating access of women working in the sector to new technologies and training them on the use of technologies as well as to train them to be agents of climate sustainable agriculture themselves; working towards enhancing women's sense of responsibility for mitigating the risks of climate change.

Gender and Climate Change

25. Climate change presents one of the biggest threats to development, and its widespread effects will affect the most vulnerable groups specially in developing countries. In Jordan, climate change is expected to have varied impacts on its climate, natural resources and local communities. The National Adaptation Plan assesses that climate change will have a significant impact on the country’s economy and its socio-economic impacts. The poor in rural areas in Jordan are expected to face the most severe consequences of climate change. They include women-headed households and families with children under 18 years old, as well as people with disabilities that depend on natural resource management. The expected impacts of climate change, particularly reduced agricultural productivity and water availability threaten livelihoods and food security for these groups. These are also the same groups who are the most vulnerable group to the impacts of climate change and have to be prioritized when developing adaptation measures. Climate change is expected to exacerbate current gender inequalities.

Table 2: Potential Socioeconomic Factors resulting in higher climate vulnerability

| Household or Individual Vulnerability | Contextual Vulnerability (National, regional or community level) |
| --- | --- |
| Increase cost of living, decrease purchasing power parity (PPP) per capita income | Food Security (Food Availability, Production Productivity, Affordability) |
| Child mortality, childhood disability, morbidity | Food Access and Distribution, Food Price Instability |
| Poverty, unemployment | Nutrition |
| Social Welfare (home appliances, air conditions etc.) | Gender inequality |
| Rural Migration (urbanization) | Rural poverty and damage due to Natural Disaster |

26. Jordan ranks amongst the top ten water poor countries in the world, and this situation is likely to be exacerbated in the future, with dire consequences for agriculture, food security, and sustainable livelihoods**. The Initial National Communication to the UNFCCC has concluded that Jordan will experience decreasing rainfall and irregular water availability, increased frequency of droughts, floods, heat waves, sandstorms, and dust storms. Due to the expected changes in climate and decreasing water availability, water scarcity is likely to increase, leading to a greater risk of conflict over water resources. The lack of equity across income classes disproportionately affecting already vulnerable people. The availability of water for agricultural activities will also be affected. Water scarcity has led to the abandonment of some areas that are no longer suitable for agriculture or farming, which has again led to an increase in diseases related to water-borne bacteria and parasites. In addition, the Jordanian economy is heavily dependent on historically rain-fed into irrigated farming. The impact of climate change for Jordan is disproportionate to the Kingdom’s emissions; as the ecosystem is heavily dependent on the availability of rainfall and groundwater and hydrological systems are at their risks and has been taking active measures to combat the effects of climate change.

27. The NAP recognises that depletion of natural resources and decreasing agricultural productivity will have a negative impact on women, who are more vulnerable than men in terms of access to available water and land for farming activities. Therefore, it is essential to mainstream gender considerations into all national policies integral to the climate change adaptation plan** Jordan developed a Climate Change Gender Action Plan (CCGAP) with the support of IUCN. The main objective of the CCGAP was to ensure that women’s voices are heard and their needs addressed through mainstream gender considerations so that women and men can have access to, participate in, contribute to, benefit from, and influence climate change adaptation policies, programs, policies and funds**. The major priority sectors of CCGAP include water, energy, agriculture and food security, waste reduction and management. It lists examples of various activities undertaken by the Jordanian government to mainstream gender considerations into women’s sector, as a fundamental part of mitigating climate change risks. It recognises that women are more vulnerable than men in terms of their dependence on agriculture to secure their livelihoods and are thus more vulnerable to climate changes.

28. The Jordan Climate Change Gender Action Plan (CCGAP) report insert the government’s commitment to mainstream gender considerations into all national policies response to climate change and to create a permanent seat for women's groups at its national climate change decision-making body. Some of the many innovative ideas on how to engage women in climate change adaptation include: providing training workshops on the enforcement of construction codes and reducing carbon footprints by supporting women’s groups in the development of green buildings; promoting women’s participation in small-scale enterprises and food processing transformation industries; and conduct “training of trainers” sessions for community centre representatives, school teachers and imams.

29. The National Climate Change Policy of the Hashemite Kingdom of Jordan (2013-2020) recognises that "women make crucial contributions in agriculture and rural enterprises in any country and are more vulnerable to climate change than men because their negative impacts of climate change will be the most felt by them". According to a study


---
**Page 137**
---

by the UNHCR, women "play a significant role" in attending to the environmental impacts of climate change and increasing communities' abilities to adapt to the increasing volatility caused by climate change[10]. Women have critical roles as educators, caretakers, practitioners, and agents of change in climate resilience. As the main custodians of water resources, food security, household energy management and household sanitation and waste management; their role is fundamental to all efforts at adaptation and mitigation. Women are also the principal caregivers of animals, home gardens, and decision-makers about household nutrition and make the majority of decisions on consumption for the household.

30. A study conducted in Jordan[11] found that women in rural communities have the following findings: rural women's existing theoretical knowledge and understanding of climate change and adaptation remain limited, impeding their ability and willingness to act and find long-term solutions; they are aware of the need to develop new strategies and coping mechanisms to deal with changing climate conditions, but they are not yet capacitated to do so; they have a strong desire for information on sustainable adaptation strategies; rural women consider the lack of awareness raising, networking opportunities and leadership capacities as the main obstacle to their participation in addressing climate change. Women also expressed that they need more time to reflect regarding their role, real or prospective, in decision-making for climate change adaptation, limiting their ability to advance community resilience as potential actors of change.

31. This study came to the following conclusions: (i) In rural communities, empowering women is a key strategy for building community resilience and adaptation capacity; support the evolution of a new generation of female champions and advocates of climate change knowledge and resilience; (ii) the increased participation and leadership of women in the labor force and their role as decision-makers in household consumption patterns to gain power and influence in gender and climate change policies and adaptation plans at the local, national and international levels; (iii) The development of a National Adaptation Plan is a new opportunity to bridge the gap by empowering women to involve in decision-making, developing gender-inclusive policies and strategies, and fostering the inclusion of women's voices into policy dialogue on climate change adaptation; by building on rural women’s existing expertise and ability to contribute to develop a common understanding of climate change and its impacts, and by supporting sustainable and well-designed gender action plans fostering rural women's capacities to act and make significant achievements in adapting to climate change; (iv) Strengthening the role of women as agents of change is essential for developing gender-sensitive strategies and policies of adaptation to climate change.

32. Women-Headed Households: Estimates in 2019 report that about 4% of the households located in rural areas[12]. According to a study by UN Women, 58% of Jordanian women are more vulnerable to the effects of climate change than men are, particularly because women constitute the majority of the labor force and are dependent for their livelihood on natural resources that are threatened by climate change. In the country, almost 91% of female-headed households are food insecure or vulnerable to food insecurity[13]. Women face significant social, economic, and political barriers that negatively affect coping capacities. Women changed

with the responsibility to secure water, food, and fuel for cooking and heating face the greatest challenges. When coupled with unequal access to resources, barriers to decision-making processes and limited mobility, women in rural areas are placed in a position where they are disproportionately affected by climate change.

The National Adaptation Plan (NAP) of Jordan is of critical importance for gender-sensitive strategies to be identified and developed to respond to the environmental and humanitarian crises caused by climate change.30

# Key Initiatives in Climate Change with a Gender Focus

## Water Wise Women: The German Federal Ministry for Economic Cooperation and Development (BMZ) Initiative to Promote Gender Equality in Water and Irrigation in Promoting Empowerment and Economic Empowerment of Women in the Water Sector.

Although water management systems are often gender-neutral, girls and boys and women are highly educated, social norms and traditions restrict their access. The Water Wise Women initiative focused on increasing women's agency and breaking barriers to their participation in water management at household level. Most women and did not recognize women’s key role in water management inside the house. The initiative aimed to empower women by providing them with knowledge about water use in the household and community level and sought to develop an approach which would lead to sustainable, efficient water use by engaging women as change agents. It empowered women through training on appropriate technologies, resources and water saving techniques.

The Water Wise Women are well-educated volunteer women that have been trained a pool of knowledge in all relevant key aspects of sustainable water management from issues of efficient water use and saving, health and hygiene, to communication with water providers. Up to 200 women of all ages and education backgrounds were qualified as Water Wise Women trainers and facilitators. They then went back to their communities and spread the message and appropriate tools. The initiative reached over 1500 households in rural areas where women interested have the opportunity to take a plumbing course and receive a repair kit with the required tools.

Results: Households in the target areas are increasingly aware of water saving and management techniques such as grey water re-use, house-garden drop irrigation, and basic hygiene. Women plumbers were supported to acquire additional licenses from the Jordanian Water Authority (JWA) and the Ministry of Water and Irrigation (MWI). They are plumbers and establish Water Wise Women Plumber Cooperatives (WWPC). WWPC has a training center where women can learn plumbing skills, which they can use outside the house before or generated any money. The support generated from governmental institutions and private sector was used to launch the Jordanian Women Plumbing Campaign under the umbrella of the Ministry of Water and Irrigation, Jordanian NGOs and local cooperation willing to adopt the same concept to their societies.

## Lessons learned: The following are the key lessons learnt from this initiative:

- **(1) Change agents need to be trained in the key message, and provision of needed information and tools:** (2) change agents need to be empowered with the skills they need and “practice what they preach.” (3) change agents who themselves are affected are more effective than outsiders. (4) Interventions for behavior change need to match the local context and should be affordable, practical and demonstrate visible results over a short time span.


---
**Page 140**
---

40. **Strengthening Women Leadership in Agricultural and National Advocacy in Jordan (2016-2017):** UN Women prepared a policy brief in partnership with ACTED with funding from the Government of Japan on Rural Women and Climate Change in Jordan. This brief is based on a field study on rural women leaders in three governorates: Karak, Ma'an, and Tafila. In 2016 in cooperation with Arab Women's Organization and the Center for Women Development in Jordan, UN Women conducted focus group discussions with women leaders from Irbid, Ma'afir, and Amman, with whom 30 semi-structured interviews were conducted. The brief also draws on the results of 10 focus group discussions held by the Ministry of Agriculture in Jordan and 25 women involved in climate change, including the Ministry of Agriculture, the Ministry of Environment, the Royal Society for Conservation of Nature, the Union for Conservation of Nature, and the National Center for Agricultural Research and Extension, among others (findings discussed under Climate Change and Gender section).

41. **USAID Takamul - Jordan Gender Program (2014-2019):** This is a five-year program funded by USAID and implemented by International Research & Exchange (IREX). The program aims to promote gender equality at the local, national, and community levels. The objectives of the program are to expand social dialogue on gender issues in Jordan; increase women's participation and advocacy for female empowerment. Project activities involved supporting gender equity efforts of the Government of Jordan, national dialogue on diverse gender equality issues, capacity building for women leaders and women's groups in the public sector, support networks of leaders and gender champions and provide financial resources to women-led organizations working throughout Jordan. The program organized the first ever Gender and Climate change conference in Jordan.

42. **Programme Mainstreaming Gender in Climate Change Efforts in Jordan (2016-2017):** Climate change has a differential impact on women and men. Existing social conditions render women more vulnerable than their male counterparts to climate change impacts, but at the same time, women in society, women have the potential to be powerful agents of change – capable of presenting solutions that can help mitigate the effects of climate change. Recognizing the important role that women can play in this regard, the Government of Jordan requested the IUCN to assist in the drafting of a gender sensitive Programme for mainstreaming gender in climate change efforts with particular emphasis on financial support of the Government of Finland. The main objective was to ensure that national and local policies and strategies related to climate change operations so that women and men can have access to, participate in, contribute to and hence optimally benefit from climate change initiatives, programs, policies and funds. The programme aimed at ensuring that all actors involved as implementers, would play to initiate and facilitate efforts - both internally, as well as externally – to ensure the integration of gender perspectives at national levels - to overcome constraints and take advantage of opportunities to promote gender equality in the context of climate change contest. The Program furthermore created a framework for integrating a gender perspective in climate change efforts in Jordan over the period.


15


---
**Page 141**
---

43. USAID is supporting a Gender Unit in the Ministry of Water and Irrigation. It has conducted a study on the status of women in the water sector. The study recommended developing a gender response planning and management system with the objective of enhancing training opportunities, better positions in leadership roles to ensure that women are able to participate effectively, and increasing the number of women to be efficient in the workplace. USAID plans to support the development of a Gender Strategy for the Ministry.

45. UN Women is currently working with the Ministry of Environment to mainstream gender in Climate Change. This involves reviewing the Climate Change Strategy from the gender perspective and developing a gender action plan for climate change within the Ministry. The centerpiece of this infrastructure will be an Internal Gender Monitoring Committee with representation from all the units in the Ministry. UN Women plans to provide training on gender equality, women’s empowerment, and help it develop guidelines for mainstreaming gender in all MoE's projects.

46. Healthy Ecosystems for Rangeland Development (HERD) (2017-2021). Sustainable land management practices are being promoted through the Climate Change Mitigation (EGC) project implemented by IUCN. Gender mainstreaming is a key component of this project, with emphasis on equal and equitable participation for development planning and implementation, as well as ensuring participation of women and other vulnerable groups in project implementation and community decision making processes. The project also aims to raise awareness raising in (i) gender responsive participatory approach in identification of development priorities, (ii) gender responsive approaches in the engagement of other vulnerable groups in the community decision making process such as water user committees, village development committees, etc., (iii) gender responsive monitoring and evaluation frameworks, and (iv) gender responsive capacity building, mobilization, management and leadership skills, including training in micro-projects identification and implementation. The project is also a component of the GCF Landscape in the Rift Valley which will focus on climate change, drylands, water harvesting and mainstream gender. It is currently implementing the HERD project which involves women in livelihood activities and rehabilitation of rangelands.

Women in Agriculture

46. A study conducted by UN Women found that women are engaged across all aspects of agricultural production including crop production, livestock production, and home-based agriculture. Home-based agriculture was the most prevalent agricultural activity amongst women, with as many as 73 percent only taking part in this, along with other small scale activities such as animal husbandry and poultry rearing. A majority (80%) of the women were engaged in paid agricultural labour, as opposed to home-based agriculture. Women who are involved in paid agricultural work tend to be younger among women from poorer economic backgrounds, who are employed informally on commercial farms undertaking medium to large-scale production. 90% Women engaged in agricultural activities were relatively young, married with children, and had low levels


---
**Page 142**
---

47. The most significant factors motivating women to engage in agriculture were economic factors (income generation and/or the need to produce food for household consumption), followed by family background (e.g., having a father or brother who is already working on farms as a hobby) and having background knowledge as well as experience in the sector. Economic factors were found to be especially important for women living in rural areas, while family background was more significant among non-farming factors such as income generation and the need to meet household expenses as well as for women with other alternatives or who are not trained to engage in other types of income generating activities.

48. In this study, agricultural areas within the target governments of Ishtid, Mafrag, Balqa and Karak were selected based on their location, climate conditions, type of soil, and type of agricultural activity; similarities in climatic conditions and shared ecological geographic features - Al-Ghor (Jordan Valley), The Highlands, and the Badia and Desert regions. These areas are characterized by a relatively high rainfall rate, fertile soils, and availability of arable land. Although demographic characteristics of women engaged in agriculture vary across these regions, they generally engage in similar types of activities they engage in very depending on the climatic region. Figure 1 depicts the different types of agricultural activity undertaken by women, as reported by the UN Women.

Figure 1: Type of agricultural activity undertaken by women, by zone

| | Northeast | Jordan Valley | Rainfed Highlands |
|---|---|---|---|
| Home-based agriculture only | 0% | 56% | 48% |
| Paid agricultural labour | 23% | 17% | 29% |
| Both home-based and paid | 77% | 27% | 23% |

Source: UN Women’s Household Women’s Empowerment Questionnaire

49. The highest percentage of women taking part in both home-based and paid agricultural activity were those in the geographical area of the Jordan Valley, the primary agricultural production area in the country, where agriculture is the main source of income for many families. Although women are underrepresented in the agricultural sector as a whole, it is mostly women from vulnerable backgrounds with less economic power who are engaged in agriculture and often as Syrian refugees women. Women-headed households are often much more vulnerable.

50. The study concluded that women’s engagement in agricultural activities has economic importance for their household. Respondents of this study who were already engaged in agricultural labour and home-based agriculture perceived their involvement to be essential or important for their household. Respondents of this UN women study further reported that the income generated from these activities was sufficient to support this engagement enhanced their ability to independently take decisions within

### Gender Profile of the Four Governorates (Karak, Madaba, Tafeth and Maan)

51. The total population of the four governorates is estimated to be about 640,900, or 328,991 households on an average household size of 5.1. Karak has a population of 350,000, Madaba 209,200, Tafeth 106,500 and Maan at 175,200. It is estimated that women constitute about 48% of the total population in these four governorates. The percentage of people living in rural areas is 34.7%. According to data from the 2016 Agriculture Census, there were 29,267 individual agriculture holders in the four Governorates with an average household size of 5.1. About 8% of these households are women-headed and 10% as a source of income. The households in the area practice a mixed farming system and 36% rely on crops and livestock

## Marital Status: In Jordan, 56 percent of women and 40 percent of men age 15-49 are married. Three percent of women and less than 1% of men are divorced or separated, and 2 percent of women and less than 1 percent of men are widowed. Early marriage increases the risk of maternal mortality and has a negative impact on the health and lives of young women. In the four Governorates, the average age at which women get married is as follows: Madaba 23.7, Karak 23.9, Tafila 22.8, and Maan 21.5.

## Mobile phones: Nine out of ten women (92%) and 89% of men own a mobile phone. Ninety-two percent of urban women and 88% of rural women own a mobile phone. Regionally, Madaba has the highest percentage of women owning a mobile phone. The number of women who own a mobile phone in the four Governorates are as follows: Madaba 63.5%, Karak 51.7%, Tafila 49.0% and Maan 28.0%.

59. Women’s Empowerment: A statistical tool known as the “Regional Gender Equality Index” (RGEI®) is used to measure the extent of women empowerment at the government level. This tool is based on 17 indicators related to women’s health, economic activity, situation at work, participation in public life, population distribution by sex and age, education, and political and social participation of women in all governorates. The Regional Gender Equality Index (RGEI®) for Karak reached the value of 2.98 in the first quarter among the governorates, the value 3 to the indicators in the second quarter, the value of its components in the third quarter was 4.05 and in the fourth quarter it reached 4.

60. Madaba came first among the governorates of the Kingdom in 2013 in the empowerment of women according to the indicators adopted in the Index, with the value of 7.98. The increase in the average age of marriage for females (from 15 years) and the increase in the average age of marriage for females, in addition to its proximity to the capital where many private and public universities are available, have helped to achieve this result. In Madaba, women’s empowerment reached a value of 82% among women with a university degree. Tafila also saw an increase in the rate of women joining the labor market, ranking first among the governorates in the proportion of economically active women (79%). The average age at marriage for females was 16 years and ranked top quartile of governorates in both the proportion of women members of governing bodies of charities and women members and the proportion of women members in council to man member ratio.

61. Tafila, recorded 2.67 as the value of the Regional Gender Equality Index. Tafila gained a high score for its indicators related to women’s education (85%), with a university degree. Tafila also saw an increase in the rate of women joining the labor market, ranking first among the governorates in the proportion of economically active women (79%). The average age at marriage for females was 16 years and ranked top quartile of governorates in both the proportion of women members of governing bodies of charities and women members and the proportion of women members in council to man member ratio.

Part II: Gender Action Plan

The Gender Action Plan has been developed in alignment with the Kingdom of Jordan’s policies and strategies to promote gender equality in general (2020-2035 National Development Strategy, 2019), the Hashemite Kingdom of Jordan Climate Change Strategy (2013-2020)) and more specifically the national strategies to address the impacts of climate change (National Adaptation Plan, February 2020; National Strategy and Action Plan for Climate Change, 2015-2020). The National Climate Change Policy of the Hashemite Kingdom of Jordan (2013) also recognizes gender equality as a key element. These policies recognize that promoting gender equality and empowering women is integral to climate change adaptation and that, whereas some have critical roles as educators, caretakers, and producers in their households and communities, they are often left out on a strategic partnership with women. They emphasize the disproportionate effects of climate change on women and girls, including (i) increasing vulnerability due to women’s knowledge of climate change; (ii) increase women’s participation in decision-making processes related to adaptation strategies and access to climate adaptive technologies for women working in agriculture; (iii) prepare a new generation of women champions and advocates of climate; (iv) provide networking opportunities for women to share experiences, lessons learned, and leadership capacities to advocate for gender-sensitive strategies and policies of adaptation to climate change; (v) define gender-sensitive strategies and policies on adaptation to climate change.

The GAP focuses on women’s visibility and agency as farmers, primary managers of water at the household and more broadly climate change agents at the community, government, and national levels. Given that women are often excluded from socially constructed gender roles and social status, women and men experience the impacts of climate change differently. The Gender Action Plan (GAP) is a two-year project that adopts a two-pronged approach to mainstreaming gender in the design and implementation of climate change adaptation strategies. The first objective undertaken by the project are gender inclusive and the second is to explore how specific activities can be included to enhance women’s agency in dealing with climate change risks by including them as key actors in decision-making processes, providing leadership directly supporting women under the Gender Action Plan contract for USD 7.64 million or 23% of total budget and 31% of GCF funding (Table 1: Creditor Budget).

The project recognizes that women-headed households are particularly vulnerable to climate change impacts due to their limited access to resources, respect their roles and responsibilities at the domestic level as the main users of the water resources for domestic use and at the farm level in their role on the homesteaded plots, backyard gardens, and livestock. The project will work with these women by using these roles by ensuring that women are a key participant in the decision regarding the roof-top water harvesting systems and the selection of beneficiaries for the Solar Water Heater Field Schools. In addition, the project recognizes that women-headed households are especially vulnerable to climate change impacts due to their limited access to resources to adapt to them. Thus woman-headed households will be given a priority in terms of their inclusion and selection in project activities especially in providing them roof-top water harvesting systems and solar water heaters. The project will implement Activity 1.1.14, the project will contract a service provider to select beneficiaries and identify women-headed households. As per description of the activity, the service provider will design a specific methodology. This will require the approval of the PNU and therefore of the national.


---

# Climate Wise Women

A cadre of 400 women will be trained as Change Agents for Climate Adaptation for climate adaptive practices from the rural areas in the four target Governorates. These young women will be advocates and repositories of knowledge on climate change, water resources management, and adaptation, anchored in rural communities. These young women will have the profile and visibility to influence policy makers and other stakeholders towards climate change. The women will be trained and certified through a customized sixteen-week course delivered over the course of a year in state-of-the-art techniques for climate change communication advocacy. Service providers at the target Governorates will ensure availability of accommodation in Governorates as well as safe and convenient arrangements for transport and training.

## Project Activities

### 68.
The project responds to national gender equality objectives through a range of activities. It has several aimed at enhancing women's knowledge of climate change and climate adaptive practices such as training in water-saving, use of rainwater harvesting structures, development and validation of Landscape Resilience Index (LRI), and other climate-resilient technologies.

### 69.
The project increases skills, knowledge and access to climate adaptive technologies for women working in agriculture through climate-smart FFS customized to women's needs. Besides a gender-responsive evaluation, ongoing support for introduction to water-efficient and other climate adaptive strategies through


---
**Page 150**
---

the cadre of community-based Climate Wise Women. The project’s implementation arrangements ensure women’s safety and well-being through measures such as providing them a briefing and instructions on safety protocols, provision of safe transport and logistics support. Women will be informed that they may face OBU due to participation in any project activity and should use the project’s grievance mechanism. This will enable the project to take any action that may be necessary at the project level or through the FFS and training team. The project will also implement measures to ensure that such incidents do not happen in the future. The risks of sexual harassment will be minimized by ensuring that the FFS and training teams are aware of these Women will be minimized through providing them with safe, secure, safe locations and appropriate training lines and instructing them to move in two or three. This will also include measures for minimizing the risk of domestic violence. In some conservative areas, mixed groups may have led to reduced participation. Family members will be briefed on the project’s safety protocols and instructed not to interfere with women engaged and minimize the chances of domestic violence, where required. Measures to ensure continued safety of women will be put in place by properly briefing the women and their family members on the project’s safety protocols and ensuring reporting cases of domestic violence by project participants. This responsibility will be assumed by a host of local community-based organizations (CBOs) and other trusted institutions such as respected community leaders, providing them recourse to resources such as the Family Protection Departments and local CBOs. The project strengthens the element of sustainability through capacity building for women. It is expected that a new generation of women champions and advocates of climate-change adaptation through their participation in the project. The training will be designed so that decision-making capabilities of women will be woven into the training and approach of the Climate Wise Women cadre. The training will also include sessions on gender-based violence around gender-based issues. The women agents will also be involved in understanding the project role in the investment in roof-top water harvesting, optimization of waste water for fodder production, etc., and how these investments can contribute to climate resilience. The practices and technologies that can enhance adaptation to climate risks and enable greater access to resources such as water, energy, food, etc., will also be shared from vulnerable households to accessing resources from CSOs, inputs and supplies through linkages with the private sector and donor initiatives, etc. The project is promoting a range of new approaches for women’s empowerment in the context of climate change that have already been initiated under other projects and are within the budget of vulnerable households.

25

Networking opportunities and strengthening rural women's voices and leadership capacities to ensure their active participation in decision-making processes related to climate change is provided through Climate-Wise Women forums. These bring these young women together with other stakeholders, including government officials. These forums will increase the visibility of these young women as agents of change, allow sharing of experiences, lessons learnt, interaction with other key stakeholders and an opportunity for them to voice their concerns. The project will also ensure that it facilitates development of gender-sensitive policies and strategies by ensuring that all the policies, strategies and curricula are reviewed with a gender lens.
